# Website widget JavaScript API

> Control the website widget from your page with window.CaploWidget, sign visitors in from your own site, and listen to widget events.

The widget loader adds a small API to your page so your own code can open the widget, pass hints about the visitor, sign the visitor in, and react to widget events. Everything here runs in the visitor’s browser; nothing on this page needs a Caplo credential. ## Get the widget control [Section titled “Get the widget control”](#get-the-widget-control) 
```js
const chat = window.CaploWidget.get('<WIDGET_ID>');
```
 `window.CaploWidget` exists once the loader script has run. `get(id)` returns the control for that widget ID, or `undefined` if no widget with that ID is loaded on the page. `window.CaploWidget.protocolVersion` is `2`. The control is available as soon as the loader runs, before the widget has finished loading. Calls you make early are kept and applied when the widget mounts, so you do not need to wait for `caplo:mounted` to call `open()` or `setVisitorHints()`. Because the loader script is `async`, run your code after it loads: 
```js
function withCaplo(widgetId, callback) {
  const chat = window.CaploWidget?.get(widgetId);
  if (chat) return callback(chat);
  const script = document.querySelector(`script[data-widget-id="${widgetId}"]`);
  script?.addEventListener('load', () => callback(window.CaploWidget.get(widgetId)), { once: true });
}
```
 ## Methods [Section titled “Methods”](#methods) | Method | What it does | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `open()` | Opens the popup widget. If the widget is still loading, it opens when it mounts. | | `close()` | Closes the popup widget. | | `setVisitorHints(hints)` | Passes unverified hints about the visitor to the conversation. Pass `null` to clear them. | | `unmount()` | Removes the widget from the page and closes its connection. To show it again, add the loader script again. | | `identity.configure({ getToken, when })` | Registers your page as a sign-in provider. See [Sign visitors in from your page](#sign-visitors-in-from-your-page). | | `identity.refresh()` | Tells the widget your page’s signed-in user changed. Returns a promise. | | `identity.signOut()` | Tells the widget your page signed its user out. Returns a promise. | | `addEventListener(type, listener)` and `removeEventListener(type, listener)` | Listen to `caplo:identity` and `caplo:identity-signout-requested`. | ### Open the widget from your own button [Section titled “Open the widget from your own button”](#open-the-widget-from-your-own-button) 
```js
withCaplo('<WIDGET_ID>', (chat) => {
  document.querySelector('#help-button').addEventListener('click', () => chat.open());
});
```
 ### Visitor hints [Section titled “Visitor hints”](#visitor-hints) Hints give the agent context it would otherwise have to ask for. They are never treated as identity: a visitor’s name in a hint does not let tools act on that person’s account. 
```js
chat.setVisitorHints({
  name: 'Ada',
  locale: 'en-GB',
  page: window.location.href,
});
```
 | Field | Rules | | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `name` | 1 to 80 characters, no control characters. | | `locale` | A BCP 47 language tag, such as `en-GB` or `fr`, up to 35 characters. | | `page` | An `http` or `https` URL without a user name or password, up to 512 characters. | Other fields are ignored. A value that breaks these rules throws a `TypeError`; if you set it before the widget has loaded, the widget fails to load and shows **Chat unavailable · Try again**. Check values before you pass them, and leave a field out rather than sending an empty string. Each call replaces the previous hints. Hints travel with the visitor’s messages, so call `setVisitorHints` again when the page changes in a single page app. ## Sign visitors in from your page [Section titled “Sign visitors in from your page”](#sign-visitors-in-from-your-page) If visitors are already signed in to your site, your page can sign them in to the widget too. This uses a **Host sign-in** connection that you set up under **Settings** then **Customer identity**, with these settings on the website entry point: * **Customer verification** allows verified visitors, and the host sign-in connection is one of the **Sign-in methods**. * **Consent mode** is **Show a notice after verification**. With the agreement mode, host sign-in does not run automatically. Your backend signs a short-lived assertion for the signed-in user; the browser never holds your signing key. See [Website widget settings](/channels/website-widget/settings/#customer-verification). 
```js
chat.identity.configure({
  when: 'open',
  getToken: async ({ nonce, requirement }) => {
    const response = await fetch('/support/caplo-token', {
      method: 'POST',
      headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' },
      body: JSON.stringify({ nonce, requirement }),
    });
    return response.status === 200 ? response.text() : null;
  },
});
```
 `getToken` receives: | Field | Meaning | | ------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `nonce` | A one-time value your backend must put in the assertion. | | `requirement` | `null`, or the level the conversation needs: `level` (`low`, `standard` or `high`), optionally `maxAuthAgeSeconds`, and for a confirmation of a specific action, `action` with an `id`, `summary` and `digest`. | Return the signed assertion as a string, or `null` when nobody is signed in on your page. The widget waits up to 30 seconds for an ordinary sign-in and up to 5 minutes when a specific action needs confirmation. `when` decides when the widget asks for a token: | `when` | The widget signs the visitor in | | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | | `open` (default) | When the widget opens, or when the visitor sends a message. | | `first-message` | When the visitor sends their first message. | | `required` | Only when the channel or an action requires verification. | Call `identity.refresh()` after your user signs in or switches account, and `identity.signOut()` after they sign out. Both return promises. Caution Never put assertions in URLs, logs or browser storage. Treat them like passwords: each one proves who the visitor is. ## Events [Section titled “Events”](#events) The loader dispatches two events on its `<script>` element. They bubble, so you can listen on `document`: | Event | When | `event.detail` | | --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `caplo:mounted` | The widget has loaded and rendered. | `{ installationId, bundleId, designId, revision }`: the widget ID, the widget release, and the design and revision shown. | | `caplo:error` | The widget could not load, so the page shows a **Chat unavailable · Try again** button instead. | `{ installationId, code }`, with `code` `"presentation-unavailable"`. | Listen to identity events on the control: | Event | When | `event.detail` | | ---------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | | `caplo:identity` | The visitor’s verification state changed. | `{ state, level?, reason? }` | | `caplo:identity-signout-requested` | A visitor signed in through your page chose to sign out in the widget. | `{}` | `state` is `anonymous`, `verified`, `reauthenticate` (the visitor must verify again) or `ended` (the verified conversation ended). `level` is present when `state` is `verified`. `reason` explains the change when there is one, for example `signed_in`, `stepped_up`, `signed_out` or `host_signed_out`. 
```js
document.addEventListener('caplo:error', (event) => {
  console.warn('Caplo widget unavailable', event.detail);
});


chat.addEventListener('caplo:identity', (event) => {
  console.log('Visitor is now', event.detail.state);
});


chat.addEventListener('caplo:identity-signout-requested', async () => {
  await signOutOfMySite();
  await chat.identity.signOut();
});
```
 The widget also dispatches `caplo:open` and `caplo:close` (no detail) on its own element when a visitor opens or closes the popup. They bubble to `document`. AI prompt Open the Caplo widget from my own button Wires an existing button on my site to open the website widget and passes visitor hints such as name, locale and page. # Website widget settings

> Configure a website entry point's allowed origins, text and voice modes, customer verification and visitor file uploads.

Entry point settings decide where the widget may run and how visitors talk to the agent. They are separate from the [widget design](/channels/website-widget/design/), which only controls appearance. To open them, go to **Channels**, open the website channel and select **Configure & install**. Settings are on the **General** and **Conversation** tabs. Select **Save entry point** to apply your changes; **Discard** drops them. Saving keeps the same widget ID, so your embed code does not change. You need the Owner, Admin or Builder role to change these settings. ## General [Section titled “General”](#general) | Setting | What it does | | ----------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Entry point name** | Shown in the console only. | | **Allowed website origin** | The exact origins that may show the widget, up to 10. Use HTTPS, or HTTP on `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or a `.localhost` host. Select **Add origin** for more. See [Allowed origins](/channels/website-widget/install/#allowed-origins). | | **Enable this website entry point** | Turn off to stop the widget accepting new conversations without deleting the channel. | | **Remove entry point** | Deletes this website channel and its installation. The agent and the widget design are kept. | The **Appearance** card on the same tab is where you apply a published design revision. Applying takes effect immediately and is not part of **Save entry point**. ## Text and voice [Section titled “Text and voice”](#text-and-voice) The widget supports text chat and, optionally, voice conversations in the browser. Voice is off for a new website channel unless you selected **Allow voice conversations** when creating it. To turn it on later: 1. On the channel page, select **Edit channel**. 2. Select **Allow website voice**, then **Save changes**. 3. In the entry point’s **Conversation** tab, set **Conversation modes** to **Text + voice** and select **Save entry point**. | Conversation mode | Visitors can | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Text only** | Chat by text. | | **Text + voice** | Chat by text and start a voice conversation from the widget. The browser asks for microphone access the first time. | Turning **Allow website voice** off switches every website entry point of the channel back to text only. ### Voice engine and turn-taking [Section titled “Voice engine and turn-taking”](#voice-engine-and-turn-taking) A website voice conversation uses the voice settings of the agent release the channel serves: voice language, speech recognition model, voice, optional realtime model, and timing such as **Start replies early**. If the agent has no voice settings, Caplo uses its default English speech recognition and voice, and no setup is needed. You change these on the agent, under **Advanced settings** then **Voice** in its configuration, and they reach the website after you publish a release and update the channel. See [Voice and speech](/providers/voice-and-speech/). ## Conversation actions [Section titled “Conversation actions”](#conversation-actions) **Conversation actions** lists the actions this entry point allows, such as **Transfer to a human**. An action is available only when the agent release also allows it. For a transfer, choose the **Transfer destination** from your handoff destinations. See [Handoff and end actions](/agents/handoff-and-end-actions/). ## Customer verification [Section titled “Customer verification”](#customer-verification) Customer verification lets visitors prove who they are, so the agent’s tools can act on their own account. You set up the verification methods themselves as connections under **Settings** then **Customer identity**: **Host sign-in** (your website’s backend signs a short assertion for a visitor it already knows), **Provider sign-in** (your OpenID Connect provider in a popup) or **Verification code** (a one-time code sent to the visitor’s email or phone on file). On the entry point you choose which of them this website offers. | **Customer verification** | Behavior | | ---------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Anonymous visitors only** | No verification is offered. | | **Visitors may verify who they are** | Visitors can chat anonymously and verify when they or a tool need it. | | **Visitors must verify before chatting** | Visitors verify before the agent answers. | When verification is allowed, select **Choose sign-in methods and consent for this channel** to set: * **Sign-in methods**: up to 4 connections, in the order the widget offers them, each with an optional button label. Host sign-in (your own page passing a signed token) runs on its own when your page registers a token provider with the [JavaScript API](/channels/website-widget/javascript-api/#sign-visitors-in-from-your-page). * **Consent mode**: **Ask for agreement before the first verification**, or **Show a notice after verification**. Host sign-in only runs automatically with the notice mode. * **Consent version** and **Consent text**: the text visitors agree to, in English and any translations you add. Change the version whenever the text changes; visitors agree to a specific version. * **Previous conversation**: **Never show earlier conversations**, or **Show the most recent one after verification**. If the agent’s tools declare verified operations, **Verified tool operations** lets you raise the verification level an operation needs, require recent authentication, and send your API a customer context token. See [Customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/). ## Visitor files during handoff [Section titled “Visitor files during handoff”](#visitor-files-during-handoff) While a person from your team handles the conversation, visitors can attach files for them. The agent never sees these files, and each file is checked for type and malware. Select **Let visitors send files during a handoff**, then choose: * **Accepted files**: **Images (JPEG, PNG, GIF, WebP)** and **PDF documents**. Both are selected by default. * **Largest file**: 1 MB, 5 MB (the default) or 10 MB. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Install the website widget](/channels/website-widget/install/) * [JavaScript API](/channels/website-widget/javascript-api/) * [Conversations and handoff](/concepts/conversations-and-handoff/)

# Troubleshoot the website widget

> Find out why the website widget does not appear, shows Chat unavailable, is blocked by a Content Security Policy, or cannot use the microphone.

Start with the browser’s developer tools on the page that should show the widget. The **Console** shows script and Content Security Policy errors, and the **Network** tab shows the widget’s requests to `api.caplo.si`. ## See why the widget failed [Section titled “See why the widget failed”](#see-why-the-widget-failed) When the widget cannot load, the loader replaces it with a **Chat unavailable · Try again** button in the bottom right corner and dispatches a `caplo:error` event. Log it to confirm which widget failed: 
```js
document.addEventListener('caplo:error', (event) => {
  console.warn('Caplo widget unavailable', event.detail);
});
```
 `event.detail` is `{ installationId, code: "presentation-unavailable" }`. For the reason, look in the **Network** tab for the `POST` request to `/v1/messaging/widget/presentation`. Its response title says what is wrong: | Response | Meaning | Fix | | -------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | 403 **Origin denied** | The page’s origin is not an allowed origin of the entry point. | Add the exact origin. See [below](#origin-not-allowed). | | 409 **Publish and apply a widget design before installing it** | No design revision is applied to the channel. | Publish a design revision and apply it. See [Design the widget](/channels/website-widget/design/). | | 503 **The selected widget release is unavailable** | The applied revision was built for a widget release that is no longer offered. | Publish a new revision of the design and apply it. | | Another status with **Widget presentation is unavailable** | The entry point is disabled, the channel is paused or deleted, the widget ID is unknown, or the service was briefly unavailable. | Check the channel and entry point in the console, then select **Try again**. | ## The widget does not appear at all [Section titled “The widget does not appear at all”](#the-widget-does-not-appear-at-all) * **The script is missing or never ran.** Check that the page source contains the loader script and that the **Network** tab shows `loader.js` loading with status 200. * **The widget ID is wrong.** The loader silently does nothing when `data-widget-id` is missing or malformed. Copy the code again from the **Install** tab. * **An inline container is missing.** For an inline design, the element named in `data-container` must exist when the loader runs. Otherwise the widget fails to load. Put the container before the script, or add the script after your app renders the container. * **The design changed between popup and inline.** If you applied a revision with a different **Display mode**, update the embed code. A popup snippet does not render an inline design. * **It is still loading.** The loader waits up to 12 seconds for the widget’s settings and 20 seconds for the widget itself before it shows **Chat unavailable**. ## Origin not allowed [Section titled “Origin not allowed”](#origin-not-allowed) The widget works only on the exact origins listed on the website entry point, under **General** then **Allowed website origin**. * Scheme, host and port must match exactly. `https://example.com` does not cover `https://www.example.com` or `https://shop.example.com`. * Paths and wildcards are not allowed. Enter `https://example.com`, not `https://example.com/support`. * HTTP only works for `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `.localhost` hosts. ## The widget is unavailable on other hosts [Section titled “The widget is unavailable on other hosts”](#the-widget-is-unavailable-on-other-hosts) A staging, preview or test site is a different origin. Either add it as an allowed origin (up to 10 per entry point) or, to keep test traffic separate, create a second website channel for your test hosts and use its widget ID there. ## Content Security Policy errors [Section titled “Content Security Policy errors”](#content-security-policy-errors) If your site sends a `Content-Security-Policy`, the browser console shows messages such as “Refused to load the script” or “Refused to connect”. Add the sources the widget needs and keep your existing ones: 
```text
script-src https://api.caplo.si;
connect-src https://api.caplo.si;
font-src https://api.caplo.si;
img-src blob:;
worker-src blob:;
```
 * A missing `script-src` stops the widget loading. * A missing `connect-src` shows **Chat unavailable**, or the widget opens but cannot send messages. * A missing `font-src` falls back to the device font. A missing `img-src blob:` hides your logo and avatar. A missing `worker-src blob:` stops map results rendering. * For voice, also allow `wss://livekit.caplo.si` in `connect-src`. * If your policy uses nonces, add `nonce="<NONCE>"` to the loader script tag. The widget applies the same nonce to its styles. The **Install** tab shows these sources under **Content Security Policy additions**. ## Voice does not start [Section titled “Voice does not start”](#voice-does-not-start) * **No voice button.** The channel must allow website voice, and the entry point’s **Conversation modes** must be **Text + voice**. See [Website widget settings](/channels/website-widget/settings/#text-and-voice). * **“Microphone access is unavailable. Check your browser permissions and audio device.”** The visitor blocked the microphone, or the browser has no input device. The visitor needs to allow microphone access for your site in the browser’s site settings and try again. * **Embedded in an iframe.** If your page shows the widget inside an iframe, the iframe needs `allow="microphone"`. * **“Voice is busy or unavailable. Please try again shortly.”** The call could not start. Try again; if it keeps happening, check the agent’s voice settings and that its speech providers are on under **Integrations** then **Service providers**. See [Voice and speech](/providers/voice-and-speech/). * **“Enable audio playback”** appears when the browser blocked sound until the visitor interacts with the page. The visitor selects it to hear the agent. ## Still stuck [Section titled “Still stuck”](#still-stuck) Collect the widget ID, the page URL, the `caplo:error` detail and the failing request from the **Network** tab, then [contact support](/resources/support/).

# Connect WhatsApp

> Connect a WhatsApp Business number through Meta, serve an agent release on it, and send approved templates when the 24-hour customer service window has closed.

A WhatsApp channel answers messages sent to one of your WhatsApp Business numbers. Caplo uses Meta’s WhatsApp Cloud API: you connect your WhatsApp Business account once under **Integrations**, then create a channel that serves a published agent release on one of its numbers. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * A Meta business portfolio and a WhatsApp Business account, or permission to create them during setup. You sign in to Meta with a Facebook account that can manage them. * A phone number for WhatsApp. It is either already on the WhatsApp Cloud API, or a new number that can receive a verification code by SMS or call. * A workspace role that can manage integrations (Owner, Admin or Developer) to connect the account, and one that can configure channels (Owner, Admin or Builder) to create the channel. * A published agent release. See [Publish an agent](/agents/publishing/). * A browser that allows popups from the console, because Meta’s signup opens in a popup. Caution Numbers used in the WhatsApp Business app on a phone cannot be connected. Caplo does not support Business App coexistence. To use such a number, first move it off the app by following Meta’s instructions, then connect it as a new number. ## Connect your WhatsApp Business account [Section titled “Connect your WhatsApp Business account”](#connect-your-whatsapp-business-account) 1. In the console, open **Integrations** then **Communication channels**. 2. Select **Add integration**, then **WhatsApp Business**. 3. Enter an **Integration name**, for example “Customer service account”. 4. Under **How is this number set up?**, choose: * **Already on the WhatsApp Cloud API** for a number that already sends messages through the Cloud API. * **A new number for WhatsApp** for a number you are adding to WhatsApp now. 5. Select **Prepare Meta connection**. A setup row appears on the **WhatsApp** tab with the status **Awaiting authorization**. 6. Select **Continue with Meta**. In Meta’s popup, sign in, choose or create the business portfolio and WhatsApp Business account, choose or add the phone number, and grant access. For a new number, Meta sends the verification code and asks for a display name. 7. When the popup closes, the row shows **Saving authorization**, then **Checking account**. Caplo verifies the access Meta granted, the account and the number, and subscribes your account to Caplo’s webhooks. When it finishes, the console shows “Your WhatsApp account is connected.” Meta must grant Caplo the `whatsapp_business_management` and `whatsapp_business_messaging` permissions. If you clear either one in the popup, setup ends with “Meta did not grant access to this WhatsApp account.” For a new number, Caplo registers the number for messaging after setup. If the number already has a two-step verification PIN, registration waits for it: select **Check connection**, open **Numbers**, enter the six-digit PIN under **Two-step PIN** and select **Register number**. Caplo never guesses a PIN. A number shows **Ready for messages** once Meta has activated it. ## Create the WhatsApp channel [Section titled “Create the WhatsApp channel”](#create-the-whatsapp-channel) 1. Open **Channels** under **Deploy**, select **Create channel** and choose **WhatsApp Business**. 2. Enter a **Channel name**, choose the **Agent** and **Agent release**, then select **Set up connection**. 3. Choose the **WhatsApp Business account** you connected and a **Phone number**. Only numbers that are ready and not used by another channel can be selected; others show **unavailable**. Select **Continue**. 4. Choose **Who answers**: * **AI agent**: the agent answers every customer. Your team can still take over any conversation. * **Your team**: the agent stays silent, also in open conversations. Messages wait in **Conversations** for your team to take over and reply. 5. Set the **Conversation inactivity timeout (seconds)**, from 300 to 604800. The default, 86400, starts a new conversation when the customer writes again after a day of silence. 6. Select **Create channel**, then send a message to the number from your own WhatsApp to test it. A channel keeps its number. To use another number, create another channel. To serve a newer release, update the channel as described in [Channels](/channels/#deploy-a-new-release). You can pause a WhatsApp channel but not delete it. Customers on WhatsApp are not signed in, so tools that need a [verified customer](/tools/customer-aware-tools/) do not run there, and customer memory is off. A WhatsApp conversation lasts at most seven days, whatever the inactivity timeout. ## The 24-hour customer service window [Section titled “The 24-hour customer service window”](#the-24-hour-customer-service-window) WhatsApp lets a business reply freely for 24 hours after the customer’s last message. Outside that window, only approved message templates can be sent. * The agent replies only inside the window. A message that reaches Caplo after its own window has passed, for example after a Meta outage, is recorded in the transcript without a reply. * Your team’s replies from the console are also bound to the window. After it closes, the console refuses a typed reply and asks you to send an approved template. * An open conversation in **Conversations** shows **WhatsApp reply window closes in** with the time left, or **WhatsApp reply window closed. Only approved templates can be sent.** The agent never sends templates. Templates are sent by a person on your team, from a conversation they have taken over. ## Message templates [Section titled “Message templates”](#message-templates) ### Create or sync templates [Section titled “Create or sync templates”](#create-or-sync-templates) Templates you create in Meta’s WhatsApp Manager are synced to Caplo automatically every few minutes. To sync now, select **Check connection** on the account, then **Check connection and templates**. The **Templates** tab lists every template with its status. Caplo can send a template when it is approved, its category is utility or marketing, and it has a text body with optional fixed text header and footer. Templates with media, buttons or variables outside the body are listed but cannot be sent. To create a template from Caplo: 1. On the **Templates** tab, select **New template**. 2. Enter the **Template name** (lowercase letters, digits and underscores; other characters become underscores), the **Language** (for example `en_US`) and the **Purpose**: **Updates about an order, booking or account** (utility) or **Offers and promotions** (marketing). 3. Write the **Message**, up to 1024 characters. Use `{{1}}`, `{{2}}` and so on for values you fill in when sending, numbered in order. A variable cannot start or end the message. Give an **Example** for each variable for Meta’s review. 4. Optionally add a **Header** and **Footer** of up to 60 characters each, without variables. A marketing template must tell people how to opt out, for example “Reply STOP to opt out”. 5. Select **Submit to Meta for review**. Meta usually decides within minutes to a day; the template becomes sendable once it shows as approved. Authentication templates cannot be created or sent from Caplo. ### Send a template from the inbox [Section titled “Send a template from the inbox”](#send-a-template-from-the-inbox) 1. In **Conversations**, open the customer’s WhatsApp conversation and select **Take over**. 2. Open **More actions** and select **Send approved template**. 3. Choose the **Approved template** and fill in each **Body parameter**. The **Message preview** shows exactly what the customer will receive. 4. Record the customer’s opt-in for the template’s category: under **Customer message with explicit opt-in**, pick the customer’s message that gives permission, confirm the check box, and select **Record customer opt-in**. A booking request or an ordinary reply is not an opt-in. 5. Choose the recorded opt-in under **Customer opt-in** and select **Queue template**. Follow its delivery in the transcript. A template can be sent while the conversation is still open, inside or outside the window. Each body parameter is sent as one line of at most 512 characters: line breaks and repeated spaces are collapsed. An opt-in lasts until the conversation ends, and you can revoke it with **Revoke selected opt-in**. Meta charges your WhatsApp Business account for template messages under its own pricing. ## What customers see [Section titled “What customers see”](#what-customers-see) * **Replies** arrive as text from your business number. * **Suggested replies** appear as up to three reply buttons of up to 20 characters, when the reply is short enough to carry buttons. Otherwise the reply is sent as text alone. See [Replies and feedback](/agents/replies-and-feedback/). * **Tool results**: cards with a reply action become a WhatsApp list; choices become reply buttons when three or fewer fit, otherwise a list; a detail with reply actions becomes reply buttons. Other results are sent as short numbered text. See [Present results](/tools/present-results/). * **Confirmations**: before a write tool runs, the customer gets a message with the details and “Confirm to approve these details, or Cancel.”, with **Confirm** and **Cancel** buttons. Only a tap on that message’s **Confirm** approves the request, and the buttons expire after 10 minutes or when the window closes. Fields cannot be edited on WhatsApp. A confirmation WhatsApp cannot show, such as one that needs editing or a stronger sign-in, is not sent; the customer is told the request has not been confirmed and to use your website or contact your team. See [Write tools](/tools/write-tools/). * **Handoff**: WhatsApp channels have no transfer destination, so the agent cannot hand off on its own. Your team takes over from **Conversations**, or from Chatwoot when you mirror WhatsApp conversations there. See [Hand off to Chatwoot](/channels/handoff-to-chatwoot/). WhatsApp customers get no message when your team takes over or hands back; your team’s replies simply arrive from the same number. ## Media and message limits [Section titled “Media and message limits”](#media-and-message-limits) * Caplo reads text messages and taps on its own buttons and lists. Photos, videos, voice notes, documents, stickers, locations and contacts are not passed to the agent, and the customer gets no reply to them. * Replies are text only; the agent cannot send files or images on WhatsApp. * A customer message longer than 4,000 characters reaches the agent cut to its first 3,980 characters, followed by “\[message shortened]”. ## Check the connection [Section titled “Check the connection”](#check-the-connection) Select **Check connection** on the account under **Integrations** to open its status: | Tab | What it shows | | -------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Deliveries** | Each outgoing message with its status (queued, sent, delivered, read, failed or unknown) and Meta’s reason for a failure. **Check delivery evidence** looks for Meta’s receipt. **Retry delivery** is offered only when the earlier attempt is proven not to have been accepted. | | **Numbers** | Each number, its registration state, and Meta’s account health: whether Meta allows the account to send, limits it, or blocks it. | | **Templates** | Synced templates, their status and why one cannot be sent. | Caplo never resends a message whose outcome is unknown, because the customer may already have it. Until its outcome is known, later replies to that customer wait. Use **Check delivery evidence** to settle it. ## Troubleshooting [Section titled “Troubleshooting”](#troubleshooting) | Problem | What to do | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | “WhatsApp signup isn’t available for this workspace yet.” | Embedded Signup is not enabled for your workspace. Contact [support](/resources/support/). | | “The Meta popup was blocked.” or “Meta could not load.” | Allow popups for `app.caplo.si`, turn off blockers for `facebook.com`, then select **Continue with Meta** again. | | “This WhatsApp account needs the Caplo app subscription before it can connect.” | Meta refused Caplo’s webhook subscription for the account. Check in Meta Business Settings that the account is shared with the Caplo app, then select **Reconnect**. | | “Meta did not return a Cloud API number for this account.” | You chose **Already on the WhatsApp Cloud API** but the number is not on it. Start again and choose **A new number for WhatsApp**, or pick another number. | | A number shows **Needs the number’s two-step PIN** | Enter the PIN on the **Numbers** tab and select **Register number**. If you do not know it, reset it in WhatsApp Manager first. | | A number is **unavailable** when creating a channel | It is not registered yet, it is already used by another channel, it is on the WhatsApp Business app, or the account needs to be reconnected. Check **Numbers** and the account status. | | Account shows **Reconnect required** or **Authorization expired** | Meta access was revoked or expired. Select **Reconnect** and finish the Meta popup. | | “Add a payment method in Meta Business Settings to continue.” | Meta blocks the account until it has a payment method. Add one in Meta Business Settings, then select **Check connection and templates**. | | Delivery failed with `provider rejected 131047` | The customer service window had closed. Send an approved template instead. | | Delivery failed with `provider rejected 131026` | Meta could not deliver to that number, for example because it is not on WhatsApp. | | Delivery failed with `provider rejected 131049` | Meta chose not to deliver a marketing message to keep engagement healthy. Try later or rely on a customer-initiated conversation. | | The agent does not answer | Check that the channel is not paused, **Who answers** is **AI agent**, and the customer’s message is text sent within the last 24 hours. | **Disconnect** on the account stops new messages and replies for every channel that uses it; existing conversations are kept. Select **Reconnect** to use it again. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Channels](/channels/) * [Messenger and Instagram](/channels/messenger-and-instagram/) * [Hand off to Chatwoot](/channels/handoff-to-chatwoot/) * [Conversations inbox](/operate/conversations-inbox/)

# Log in and use API keys

> Log in to the caplo CLI as yourself, see where the session is kept, change CLI settings, and create API keys for CI and automation.

The CLI authenticates in one of two ways: * **As you**, after `caplo auth login`. Commands act with your workspace role, the same as in the console. * **As an API client**, when `CAPLO_API_KEY` holds an API key. Commands act with the key’s scopes. Use this in CI and other automation where nobody can log in. ## Log in [Section titled “Log in”](#log-in) 
```sh
caplo auth login
```
 1. The CLI opens your browser at the Caplo sign-in page. If it does not open, copy the URL the CLI prints. 2. Sign in as you do in the console. The browser then shows that you are logged in to the Caplo CLI, and you can close it. 3. The CLI prints your name and email. If you belong to exactly one workspace, it selects that workspace. If you belong to several, run `caplo workspace use <name>` to choose one; see [Workspaces](/cli/workspaces/). The browser returns to a short-lived listener on `127.0.0.1` on your machine, so the browser and the CLI must run on the same computer. The sign-in must finish within five minutes. ### On a machine without a browser [Section titled “On a machine without a browser”](#on-a-machine-without-a-browser) On a remote server, in a container, or over SSH, use the device flow: 
```sh
caplo auth login --device
```
 The CLI prints a URL and a code. Open the URL on any device, sign in, and confirm the code; the CLI finishes as soon as you do. `--no-browser` only stops the CLI from opening a browser itself. It still needs a browser on the same machine to finish, so prefer `--device` when there is none. ### Check and end the session [Section titled “Check and end the session”](#check-and-end-the-session) 
```sh
caplo auth status
caplo auth logout
```
 `caplo auth status` shows who you are logged in as, the API and sign-in server, where the session is stored, and the selected workspace. With `CAPLO_API_KEY` set, it shows the API client, its workspace and its scopes instead. `caplo auth logout` deletes the stored session from your machine and then ends it on the server. If ending it on the server fails, the CLI says so; the local copy is already gone. ## Where the session is stored [Section titled “Where the session is stored”](#where-the-session-is-stored) Logging in creates a long-lived session. The CLI keeps it as a refresh token and gets short-lived access tokens from it as needed; access tokens stay in memory and are never written to disk. Several `caplo` commands can run at once safely. | Storage | Where | When | | -------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `keychain` (default) | The operating system’s credential store: macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, or the Secret Service (such as GNOME Keyring) on Linux, under the service name `caplo` | Desktops and laptops | | `file` | `credentials.json` in the configuration directory, readable only by your user (mode 0600) | Machines without a credential store, such as a minimal Linux server | To use file storage, log in with `--insecure-storage`, or set it first with `caplo config set credential-storage file`. The choice is saved and applies to later logins too. Caution Anyone who can read `credentials.json` can act as you in Caplo until you log out. Use file storage only on a machine and account you control, and prefer an [API key](#api-keys-for-automation) for unattended jobs. The configuration directory is `CAPLO_CONFIG_DIR` if set, otherwise `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/caplo` or `~/.config/caplo` on macOS and Linux, and `%AppData%\caplo` on Windows. It holds `config.yaml` with your settings and selected workspace, and `credentials.json` when you use file storage. ## CLI settings [Section titled “CLI settings”](#cli-settings) `caplo config` shows and changes the settings in `config.yaml`: 
```sh
caplo config list
caplo config get api-url
caplo config set credential-storage file
```
 | Key | Default | What it is | | -------------------- | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------ | | `api-url` | `https://api.caplo.si` | The Caplo API the CLI calls. | | `issuer` | `https://auth.caplo.si/realms/caplo` | The sign-in server. | | `client-id` | `caplo-cli` | The sign-in client the CLI identifies as. | | `credential-storage` | `keychain` | Where the session is kept: `keychain` or `file`. | `caplo config list` also shows the selected workspace. You only need to change `api-url`, `issuer` or `client-id` if Caplo support gives you different values. Changing `api-url` clears the selected workspace. After changing a setting, run `caplo auth login` again. When you log in, the API and sign-in server come from, in order: the `--api-url` and `--issuer` flags, the `CAPLO_API_URL` and `CAPLO_ISSUER` environment variables, the saved settings, then the defaults above. A successful login saves them. To keep two separate logins on one machine, give each its own `CAPLO_CONFIG_DIR`. ## API keys for automation [Section titled “API keys for automation”](#api-keys-for-automation) An API client is a workspace identity for CI and scripts. It is not a member: it has no role, cannot use the console, and can only do what its scopes allow in its own workspace. Each client can have several keys, so you can rotate a key without downtime. Keys start with `caplo_sk_`. ### Who can create one [Section titled “Who can create one”](#who-can-create-one) Workspace Owners, Admins and Developers can manage API clients. You can only give a client scopes whose actions your own role holds: for example, a Developer can create a client with tool scopes but not with `agents:write` or `agents:publish`. See [Workspaces and roles](/concepts/workspaces-and-roles/). ### Create a client [Section titled “Create a client”](#create-a-client) * Console 1. Open **Settings**, then **API access**. 2. Click **Create client**, enter a **Client name**, and turn on the permissions it needs. 3. Click **Create key** and copy the key from **Copy your API key**. It is shown only once. * CLI Log in as yourself, select the workspace, then: 
  ```sh
  caplo workspace api-client create github-deploy \
    --scope agents:write,agents:publish,tools:write,tools:publish
  ```
 The key is printed once on standard output, so you can pipe it straight into your CI’s secret store. Repeat `--scope` or separate scopes with commas. Store the key as a secret in your CI system right away. Caplo keeps only a hash of it and cannot show it again. ### Scopes [Section titled “Scopes”](#scopes) A write or publish scope includes the read scope of the same resource. | Scope | Console label | Allows | | ---------------- | ---------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `agents:read` | Read agents | Read agents, releases, eval suites and runs, and knowledge bases | | `agents:write` | Edit agents and knowledge | Create agents, save drafts and eval suites, create knowledge bases and upload documents | | `agents:publish` | Publish agents and knowledge | Run evals, publish and revoke agent releases, archive and delete agents, change the workspace’s publication requirements (including turning off the eval requirement), approve documents and publish knowledge versions | | `tools:read` | Read tools | Read tool packages, builds, versions, logs, remote MCP servers and secret names | | `tools:write` | Edit tools | Create and upload packages, build them, run remote fixtures, archive and delete packages, review remote MCP server tools | | `tools:publish` | Publish tools | Publish and revoke package versions, publish remote MCP server tools, set a package’s own secret values | | `channels:read` | Read channels | Read channels and the release each serves | | `channels:write` | Deploy to channels | Point channels at a release and roll them back. It also allows creating, editing, pausing and deleting channels and editing and publishing widget designs, so grant it only to automation you trust with your channels. | The workspace also accepts `conversations:read`, `usage:read`, `endpoints:read` and `endpoints:write`, but no Caplo API accepts API keys for them yet. See [API keys](/admin/api-keys/). ### Use the key [Section titled “Use the key”](#use-the-key) Set two environment variables. The CLI then authenticates as the client for every command, instead of any login on the machine: 
```sh
export CAPLO_API_KEY=<API_KEY>
export CAPLO_WORKSPACE=<WORKSPACE_ID>
caplo auth status
```
 `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` (or `--workspace`, or a project [environment](/cli/environments/)) must name the client’s workspace by ID, not by name, because a key cannot list workspaces. The CLI checks that the key belongs to that workspace before it does anything else. `caplo workspace get` while logged in, or the console, shows the ID. The key calls `CAPLO_API_URL` if set, then the saved `api-url`, then `https://api.caplo.si`. ### What works with an API key [Section titled “What works with an API key”](#what-works-with-an-api-key) | Commands | Scopes needed | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `caplo validate`, `caplo test`, `caplo identity` | None: they do not call the Caplo API | | `caplo diff`, `caplo pull`, `caplo agent list`, `get` and `pull` | `agents:read`, plus `tools:read` when packages are involved | | `caplo push`, `caplo agent push` | `agents:write`, plus `tools:write` when the project has packages | | `caplo eval`, `caplo agent eval run` | `agents:write` and `agents:publish`, plus `tools:write` with packages | | `caplo publish`, `caplo agent publish` | `agents:write` and `agents:publish`, plus `tools:write` and `tools:publish` with packages, plus `channels:write` when the environment deploys channels | | `caplo tool` list, push, build, publish and version commands, `caplo tool secret set` and `unset` | `tools:read`, `tools:write` or `tools:publish` | | `caplo mcp list`, `get`, `tools`, `review` and `publish` | `tools:read`, `tools:write` or `tools:publish` | | `caplo secret list` and `usage`, `caplo tool secret list` | `tools:read` | | `caplo knowledge` | `agents:read`; `agents:write` to create and sync; `agents:publish` to approve and publish | | `caplo channel list` and `get` | `channels:read` and `agents:read` | | `caplo channel deploy` and `rollback` | `channels:write` and `agents:read` | These need a person’s login and do not work with an API key: * `caplo workspace` commands, including `caplo workspace api-client`: an API client cannot manage workspaces or other clients. * `caplo secret set` and `delete`, and `caplo tool secret bind` and `unbind`: managing workspace secrets needs a member with the secrets permission. * `caplo mcp add`, `update`, `remove`, `check`, `connect` and `refresh`: connecting remote MCP servers needs a member who can manage integrations. * `caplo agent chat` and `caplo agent test`: test conversations belong to a person. If the API refuses a key, the CLI says that an API key cannot do this or that it was revoked. Check the scopes with `caplo auth status`. ### Rotate a key [Section titled “Rotate a key”](#rotate-a-key) Add a new key, switch your CI secret to it, then revoke the old ones: 
```sh
caplo workspace api-client rotate github-deploy
caplo workspace api-client revoke github-deploy --except <NEW_KEY_ID_OR_PREFIX>
```
 `rotate` prints the new key once and leaves the old keys active, so nothing breaks while you update the secret. `caplo workspace api-client list` shows each client’s scopes and the prefixes of its active keys. In the console, use **Add rotation key** on the client. ### Revoke keys [Section titled “Revoke keys”](#revoke-keys) 
```sh
caplo workspace api-client revoke github-deploy
caplo workspace api-client revoke github-deploy --key <KEY_ID_OR_PREFIX>
```
 Without `--key`, every active key of the client is revoked. The command asks first; pass `--yes` in scripts. Anything still using a revoked key stops working at once. A client’s scopes cannot be changed; to change them, create a new client and revoke the old one’s keys.

# Publish from CI/CD

> Run caplo in GitHub Actions or GitLab CI with an API key to validate and test every change and publish from your main branch.

Keep your [project](/cli/projects/) in Git and let a pipeline publish it: every pull request is validated and its tool fixtures run, and every merge to `main` is evaluated and published. Nobody publishes from a laptop, and the release history matches your commit history. In CI there is nobody to log in, so the CLI authenticates with an **API key** instead. See [API keys for automation](/cli/authentication/#api-keys-for-automation) for how keys and scopes work. ## Set up the key [Section titled “Set up the key”](#set-up-the-key) 1. Log in as a workspace Owner or Admin, so you can grant agent scopes, and select the workspace you publish to. 2. Create an API client with the scopes a publish needs, and send its key straight into your repository’s secrets without printing it. With the [GitHub CLI](https://cli.github.com): 
   ```sh
   caplo workspace api-client create github-deploy \
     --scope agents:write,agents:publish,tools:write,tools:publish \
     | gh secret set CAPLO_API_KEY
   ```
 Add `channels:write` if an [environment](/cli/environments/#deploy-channels) deploys channels. Leave out the `tools` scopes if the project has no tool packages. 3. Store the workspace ID as `CAPLO_WORKSPACE`. It is not secret, but keeping it next to the key keeps them in step: 
   ```sh
   caplo workspace get -o json
   gh secret set CAPLO_WORKSPACE --body <WORKSPACE_ID>
   ```
 With an API key, the workspace must be given by ID, never by name. ## GitHub Actions [Section titled “GitHub Actions”](#github-actions) Save this as `.github/workflows/caplo.yml`. It pins the CLI version, so a new CLI release never changes your pipeline until you update the pin. 
```yaml
name: Caplo


on:
  pull_request:
  push:
    branches: [main]


permissions:
  contents: read


env:
  CAPLO_VERSION: v0.4.0


jobs:
  check:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 24
      - name: Install the Caplo CLI
        run: |
          curl -fsSL "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getcaplo/homebrew-tap/$CAPLO_VERSION/install.sh" | sh
          echo "$HOME/.local/bin" >> "$GITHUB_PATH"
      - name: Install tool package dependencies
        run: |
          for dir in tools/*/; do
            if [ -f "$dir/package.json" ]; then npm ci --prefix "$dir"; fi
          done
      - run: caplo validate
      - run: caplo test


  publish:
    if: github.event_name == 'push' && github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
    needs: check
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    concurrency: caplo-publish
    env:
      CAPLO_API_KEY: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_API_KEY }}
      CAPLO_WORKSPACE: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_WORKSPACE }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - name: Install the Caplo CLI
        run: |
          curl -fsSL "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getcaplo/homebrew-tap/$CAPLO_VERSION/install.sh" | sh
          echo "$HOME/.local/bin" >> "$GITHUB_PATH"
      - run: caplo auth status
      - run: caplo eval
      - run: caplo publish --yes
```
 What each part does: * **`check`** runs on every pull request and push. `caplo validate` and `caplo test` need no key and call no workspace, so they also run for pull requests from forks. `npm ci` needs each package’s `package-lock.json` committed. * **`publish`** runs only on pushes to `main`. `caplo auth status` prints the API client and its scopes, which makes a wrong key easy to spot in the log. `caplo eval` pushes the drafts and runs every agent’s eval cases, so a failing case stops the job before anything is published. `caplo publish --yes` then publishes bottom up, reusing a passing eval run wherever the draft has not changed since. * **`--yes`** is required: without a terminal, a publish that would ask for confirmation stops with exit code 2. It also covers the project’s first publish to the workspace. * **`concurrency`** stops two publishes from running against the same workspace at once. The install script checks the archive’s SHA-256 checksum before installing. To also gate publishing on a reviewer, put the `publish` job in a [GitHub environment](https://docs.github.com/actions/deployment/targeting-different-environments/using-environments-for-deployment) with required reviewers. Caution Do not run `caplo push`, `caplo eval` or `caplo publish` on pull requests against the workspace that serves customers. `push` and `eval` overwrite the workspace drafts with the pull request’s files, and two open pull requests would overwrite each other. Use a separate staging workspace for that, or limit pull requests to `caplo validate`, `caplo test` and `caplo diff`. ### Show the plan on pull requests [Section titled “Show the plan on pull requests”](#show-the-plan-on-pull-requests) To see what a merge would change, add a step to `check` with a key that has only `agents:read` and `tools:read`: 
```yaml
      - run: caplo diff
        env:
          CAPLO_API_KEY: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_READ_KEY }}
          CAPLO_WORKSPACE: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_WORKSPACE }}
```
 `caplo diff -o json` prints the same plan as one JSON document, to post as a comment or check with a policy; see [Plans as JSON](/cli/scripting/#plans-as-json). ### Staging and production [Section titled “Staging and production”](#staging-and-production) With [environments](/cli/environments/) in `caplo.yaml`, give each its own API client and secret, and select the environment per job: 
```yaml
      - run: caplo publish --env staging --yes
        env:
          CAPLO_STAGING_KEY: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_STAGING_KEY }}
```
 For production, run a separate job, for example on `workflow_dispatch` or a tag, that sets both keys. With `promoteFrom: staging`, the CLI reads staging with `CAPLO_STAGING_KEY` and refuses any agent staging has not released from the same source: 
```yaml
      - run: caplo publish --env prod --yes
        env:
          CAPLO_PROD_KEY: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_PROD_KEY }}
          CAPLO_STAGING_KEY: ${{ secrets.CAPLO_STAGING_KEY }}
```
 Do not also set `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` in these jobs unless it matches the environment’s `workspace` exactly; a different value stops the command with exit code 2. AI prompt Publish my Caplo project from GitHub Actions Adds a GitHub Actions workflow that validates and tests every change and publishes the Caplo project from main with an API key. Copy prompt[Open in ## GitLab CI [Section titled “GitLab CI”](#gitlab-ci) Add `CAPLO_API_KEY` and `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` under **Settings**, **CI/CD**, **Variables** in GitLab, masked and protected so only protected branches can read them. Then in `.gitlab-ci.yml`: 
```yaml
stages: [check, publish]


variables:
  CAPLO_VERSION: v0.4.0


default:
  image: node:24
  before_script:
    - curl -fsSL "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getcaplo/homebrew-tap/$CAPLO_VERSION/install.sh" | sh
    - export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"


check:
  stage: check
  script:
    - for dir in tools/*/; do if [ -f "$dir/package.json" ]; then npm ci --prefix "$dir"; fi; done
    - caplo validate
    - caplo test


publish:
  stage: publish
  resource_group: caplo-publish
  rules:
    - if: $CI_COMMIT_BRANCH == $CI_DEFAULT_BRANCH
  script:
    - caplo eval
    - caplo publish --yes
```
 ## Exit codes [Section titled “Exit codes”](#exit-codes) Pipelines stop on any non-zero exit code. The code tells you where to look: | Code | Meaning in CI | Typical fix | | ------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | | `0` | Success, including nothing to publish | | | `1` | An error, such as a refused API key, a missing scope, a network failure or a failed build | Read the error line; check scopes with `caplo auth status` | | `2` | A usage error: a missing `--yes`, a workspace given by name with an API key, `--workspace` disagreeing with `--env`, or `channels:write` missing for a channel deploy | Fix the command or the variables | | `3` | A failed gate: validation, tool fixtures, eval cases, or a promotion with no matching release | Fix the files, or publish to the `promoteFrom` environment first | | `130`, `143` | The job was cancelled | Rerun it | A publish that fails partway keeps what it already published, such as tool versions or child agent releases. Fix the cause and run it again; unchanged resources are skipped. See [Scripting](/cli/scripting/) for output formats and environment variables.

# Publish to several environments

> Define staging and production environments in caplo.yaml, vary agent settings per environment, deploy channels, and promote only what passed the previous environment.

An environment is a named workspace that a project publishes to, such as `staging` or `prod`. Keep one workspace per environment so that testing never touches what customers use. With environments, one set of agent files publishes to every workspace, with small per-environment differences, and production can be limited to exactly what staging released. ## Define environments [Section titled “Define environments”](#define-environments) Add an `environments` map to the project’s `caplo.yaml`: 
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Project
name: shop
agents: agents/*.yaml
tools: tools/*
environments:
  staging:
    workspace: <STAGING_WORKSPACE_ID>
    apiKeyEnv: CAPLO_STAGING_KEY
    channels:
      concierge: [Staging website]
  prod:
    workspace: <PROD_WORKSPACE_ID>
    apiKeyEnv: CAPLO_PROD_KEY
    promoteFrom: staging
    agents:
      concierge:
        model: {temperature: 0.2}
        suggestedReplies: null
    channels:
      concierge: [Website, twilio/Support line]
```
 | Field | Required | Description | | ------------- | -------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `workspace` | Yes | The workspace ID. A workspace name also works when you are logged in, but an [API key](/cli/authentication/#api-keys-for-automation) needs the ID. | | `apiKeyEnv` | No | The name of an environment variable that holds this environment’s API key, such as `CAPLO_PROD_KEY`. When that variable is set, the CLI uses it instead of `CAPLO_API_KEY`. | | `promoteFrom` | No | Another environment. A publish here only goes ahead for agents that environment already released from the same source. See [Promote a release](#promote-a-release). | | `agents` | No | Agent fields by agent file key, merged over the agent files. See [Vary agents per environment](#vary-agents-per-environment). | | `channels` | No | Channels by agent file key. After a publish, each listed channel is pointed at the agent’s newest release. See [Deploy channels](#deploy-channels). | Environment names start with a lowercase letter and use only lowercase letters, digits and hyphens. `promoteFrom` must name another environment and must not loop back. ## Use an environment [Section titled “Use an environment”](#use-an-environment) Pass `--env` (`-e`), or set `CAPLO_ENV`, on `caplo validate`, `diff`, `push`, `eval` and `publish`: 
```sh
caplo diff --env staging
caplo publish --env prod
```
 Selecting an environment does three things: 1. **Workspace.** The command acts on the environment’s workspace. If `--workspace` or `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` names a different one, the command stops with exit code 2 instead of guessing. 2. **Credentials.** If the environment’s `apiKeyEnv` variable is set, the command authenticates with that key. Otherwise it uses `CAPLO_API_KEY` if set, and your login if not. 3. **Agent fields.** The environment’s `agents` fields are merged over the agent files before anything is compared or pushed. Without `--env` or `CAPLO_ENV`, project commands act on the [selected workspace](/cli/workspaces/) and use the agent files unchanged. ## Vary agents per environment [Section titled “Vary agents per environment”](#vary-agents-per-environment) Under `agents`, each key is an agent file key and each value is a partial agent file. It merges over the file like a JSON merge patch: * Mappings merge key by key, so `model: {temperature: 0.2}` changes only the temperature and keeps the model name. * `null` removes a field, so `suggestedReplies: null` turns suggested replies off in that environment. * Any other value, including a list, replaces the field entirely. Use this for settings that should really differ, such as model, temperature, greeting or limits. Knowledge base and remote MCP server names already resolve in each command’s own workspace, so you rarely need to override them. `caplo validate` checks every environment’s fields merged over each file, whether or not you pass `--env`. With `--env`, it also checks the agents exactly as that environment publishes them. ## Deploy channels [Section titled “Deploy channels”](#deploy-channels) An environment’s `channels` lists, for each agent key, the channels that should serve that agent: 
```yaml
channels:
  concierge: [Website, twilio/Support line, <CHANNEL_ID>]
```
 Name a channel by its name, by its ID, or as `<kind>/<name>` when two channels share a name. Kinds are `website`, `whatsapp`, `messenger`, `instagram`, `twilio` and `telnyx`; `caplo channel list` shows yours. After `caplo publish --env <name>` publishes, it points each listed channel at its agent’s newest release, if the channel does not serve it already. That happens even when this publish made no new release. The plan lists each channel as `channel/<kind>/<name>` with a `deploy` step, and the publish confirmation covers it. New conversations get the new release; conversations already open keep theirs. Every other channel setting stays as it is. The agent must have a release in that workspace. With an API key, deploying channels needs the `channels:write` scope. The CLI checks this before it starts, so a missing scope stops the publish with exit code 2 before anything changes. ## Promote a release [Section titled “Promote a release”](#promote-a-release) `promoteFrom` makes an environment accept only what the previous one released. Each release the CLI publishes from a project records a **source digest** of the agent: * its agent file as written, byte for byte, before any environment’s fields apply * the files of the project packages it uses, as `caplo push` uploads them * the source of its project sub-agents, in the same way `caplo.yaml` and environment fields are not part of the digest, so production can use different settings and still match staging. Any edit to the agent file, a package it uses, or a project sub-agent, even a comment, changes the digest. When you publish to an environment with `promoteFrom`, every agent the publish would release must have a release in the `promoteFrom` environment with the same digest that is not revoked. The plan prints the matching release for each agent. If one is missing, the publish stops with exit code 3 before changing anything, naming the agents that need publishing there first. 1. Publish to staging and test there: 
   ```sh
   caplo publish --env staging
   ```
 2. When staging is good, publish the same commit to production: 
   ```sh
   caplo publish --env prod
   ```
 The plan shows lines such as `agent/concierge matches staging release 1.3.0`. Confirm to publish. 3. If you changed anything in between, publish to staging again first. Production refuses a source staging has not released. To read the `promoteFrom` environment, the CLI uses that environment’s own `apiKeyEnv` key when its variable is set, and otherwise your login. A key for one workspace cannot read another, so when you publish with an API key, the `promoteFrom` environment needs `apiKeyEnv` and that variable must hold a key of its workspace with at least `agents:read`: 
```sh
CAPLO_STAGING_KEY=<STAGING_KEY> CAPLO_PROD_KEY=<PROD_KEY> caplo publish --env prod --yes
```
 Caution Releases are numbered per workspace, so the same source can be 1.3.0 in staging and 2.1.0 in production. Compare environments by source digest, which the plan prints, not by version number.
