## Credentials per environment [Section titled “Credentials per environment”](#credentials-per-environment) | How you run | Workspace field | Keys | | ------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Logged in as yourself | ID or name; you must be a member of every environment’s workspace | None needed | | One API key, one environment per job | ID | `CAPLO_API_KEY`, or the environment’s `apiKeyEnv` variable | | API keys, with `promoteFrom` | ID | The target’s key in `CAPLO_API_KEY` or its `apiKeyEnv` variable, and the `promoteFrom` environment’s key in its `apiKeyEnv` variable | Each key belongs to one workspace, so create one API client per environment workspace. See [Run the CLI in CI/CD](/cli/ci-cd/) for a complete pipeline.

# Install the CLI

> Install the caplo command-line tool on macOS, Linux or Windows, check the download, and keep it up to date.

The `caplo` CLI is a single binary for macOS, Linux and Windows on ARM64 (Apple silicon, Graviton) and AMD64 (Intel, AMD). Every release is published on the [Caplo releases page](https://github.com/getcaplo/homebrew-tap/releases) with a checksum file and a signature. Running tool package fixtures on your machine (`caplo test` and `caplo tool test`) also needs Node.js 24 and the package’s dependencies installed. On Windows those commands run under WSL; everything else works natively. ## Install [Section titled “Install”](#install) * macOS With [Homebrew](https://brew.sh): 
  ```sh
  brew install getcaplo/tap/caplo
  ```
 Or with the install script, which needs no Homebrew and no `sudo`: 
  ```sh
  curl -fsSL https://cli.caplo.si/install.sh | sh
  export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"
  ```
 Add the `export` line to your shell profile (for example `~/.zshrc`) so `caplo` stays on your `PATH` in new terminals. * Linux With [Homebrew](https://brew.sh): 
  ```sh
  brew install getcaplo/tap/caplo
  ```
 Or with the install script. It needs `curl`, `tar` and `sha256sum`; on Ubuntu or Debian install them first with `sudo apt-get install curl ca-certificates tar coreutils`. 
  ```sh
  curl -fsSL https://cli.caplo.si/install.sh | sh
  export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"
  ```
 Add the `export` line to your shell profile (for example `~/.bashrc`) so `caplo` stays on your `PATH` in new terminals. * Windows Windows has no installer. Download the archive and put `caplo.exe` on your `PATH`. 1. Open the [releases page](https://github.com/getcaplo/homebrew-tap/releases) and download `caplo_<VERSION>_windows_amd64.zip` (or `windows_arm64` on an ARM device) and `checksums.txt` from the latest release. 2. [Check the archive](#verify-a-download) against `checksums.txt`. 3. Extract `caplo.exe` to a folder of your own and add that folder to your user `PATH`. In PowerShell, from the download folder: 
     ```powershell
     $dir = "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Programs\caplo"
     Expand-Archive .\caplo_<VERSION>_windows_amd64.zip -DestinationPath $dir
     $userPath = [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User")
     [Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "$userPath;$dir", "User")
     ```
 4. Open a new terminal so it picks up the new `PATH`. Use one installation method. If you install with both Homebrew and the script, two copies end up on your `PATH` and the one that runs depends on its order. ## Check the installation [Section titled “Check the installation”](#check-the-installation) 
```sh
caplo version
```
 It prints the CLI version, the commit it was built from, and the Go version and platform. `caplo --version` prints the version alone, and `caplo version -o json` prints the same details as JSON. Next, [log in](/cli/authentication/). ## Install script options [Section titled “Install script options”](#install-script-options) The script detects your operating system and architecture, downloads the matching archive and `checksums.txt` from the release over HTTPS, checks the archive’s SHA-256 hash, and only then replaces `caplo` in the install directory. It never runs `sudo` and never edits your shell profile. Set these variables on the `sh` side of the pipe: | Variable | Default | Effect | | ------------------- | -------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `CAPLO_VERSION` | `latest` | The release to install, as `vX.Y.Z`, for example `v0.4.0`. Only stable versions are accepted. | | `CAPLO_INSTALL_DIR` | `~/.local/bin` | The directory to install `caplo` into. It is created if missing. | To pin both the script and the CLI to one release, fetch the script from that release’s tag: 
```sh
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getcaplo/homebrew-tap/v0.4.0/install.sh \
  | CAPLO_VERSION=v0.4.0 CAPLO_INSTALL_DIR="$HOME/bin" sh
```
 You can also download `install.sh`, read it, and run it yourself. The same script is attached to every release. ## Verify a download [Section titled “Verify a download”](#verify-a-download) The install script and Homebrew check the SHA-256 hash for you. When you download an archive by hand, check it yourself: download `checksums.txt` from the same release into the folder that holds the archive. * macOS 
  ```sh
  shasum -a 256 --ignore-missing -c checksums.txt
  ```
 * Linux 
  ```sh
  sha256sum --ignore-missing -c checksums.txt
  ```
 * Windows 
  ```powershell
  (Get-FileHash .\caplo_<VERSION>_windows_amd64.zip -Algorithm SHA256).Hash.ToLower()
  Select-String "windows_amd64.zip" .\checksums.txt
  ```
 The two hashes must be identical. A matching line ends in `OK`. If the hash does not match, delete the archive and download it again. ### Check the signature [Section titled “Check the signature”](#check-the-signature) Each release also signs `checksums.txt` with a keyless [Sigstore](https://www.sigstore.dev) signature, so you can confirm that Caplo published the checksums and not someone else. Download `checksums.txt.sigstore.json` from the release and verify it with [cosign](https://docs.sigstore.dev/cosign/system_config/installation/): 
```sh
cosign verify-blob checksums.txt \
  --bundle checksums.txt.sigstore.json \
  --certificate-identity-regexp '@getcaplo\.com$' \
  --certificate-oidc-issuer https://accounts.google.com
```
 It prints `Verified OK`. A verified `checksums.txt` plus a matching hash means the archive is the one Caplo released. ## Update [Section titled “Update”](#update) * macOS 
  ```sh
  brew update && brew upgrade getcaplo/tap/caplo
  ```
 If you used the install script, run it again. It installs the latest release over the old binary. * Linux 
  ```sh
  brew update && brew upgrade getcaplo/tap/caplo
  ```
 If you used the install script, run it again. It installs the latest release over the old binary. * Windows Download the new archive, check it, and extract `caplo.exe` over the old one. Your login, settings and selected workspace are kept across updates. ## Uninstall [Section titled “Uninstall”](#uninstall) 1. End your session so it stops working on the server too: 
   ```sh
   caplo auth logout
   ```
 2. Remove the binary: `brew uninstall caplo` for Homebrew, or delete `caplo` from the install directory (`~/.local/bin` by default). On Windows, delete the folder that holds `caplo.exe` and remove it from your `PATH`. 3. Optionally delete the configuration directory: `~/.config/caplo` (or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/caplo`) on macOS and Linux, `%AppData%\caplo` on Windows, or the directory `CAPLO_CONFIG_DIR` names. See [Authentication](/cli/authentication/#where-the-session-is-stored) for what it holds.

# Work with a project

> Keep agents and tool packages as files in a caplo project, understand caplo.yaml, and choose what each command acts on.

A project is a folder, usually a Git repository, that holds a set of agents and the tool packages they use as plain files. The project commands (`caplo validate`, `diff`, `push`, `test`, `eval`, `publish` and `pull`) read the folder, compare it with a workspace, and create or update what changed. Review changes in pull requests, then publish from your terminal or [from CI](/cli/ci-cd/). ## Create a project [Section titled “Create a project”](#create-a-project) 
```sh
caplo init support
caplo init shop --template summit
```
 `caplo init [dir]` creates the project in `dir`, or in the current directory, which must be missing or empty. The project name defaults to the folder name; set another with `--name`. A name starts with a lowercase letter and uses only lowercase letters, digits and hyphens. | Template | What you get | | ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `blank` (default) | One agent with a starter eval case | | `summit` | A sample outdoor shop: a concierge agent with fast routing, three specialist sub-agents, and two tool packages with test fixtures | The `summit` template creates this layout: 
```text
shop/
  caplo.yaml                  the project file
  agents/
    concierge.yaml            one agent file per agent
    gear.yaml
    orders.yaml
    returns.yaml
  tools/
    order-desk/               one directory per tool package
      caplo.yaml              the package's own caplo.yaml
      package.json
      tsconfig.json
      src/
      tests/
    store-stock/
      ...
```
 Each tool package gets `package.json` and `tsconfig.json` from the current tool SDK templates. Run `npm install` in each package before you run its fixtures; `caplo init` prints the commands. To start from an agent that already exists in a workspace, use [`caplo pull`](#pull-an-existing-agent) instead. ## The project file [Section titled “The project file”](#the-project-file) The project’s `caplo.yaml` sits at the root of the folder: 
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Project
name: shop
agents: agents/*.yaml
tools: tools/*
```
 | Field | Required | Description | | -------------- | -------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `apiVersion` | Yes | Always `v1`. | | `kind` | Yes | Always `Project`. This is what tells it apart from a tool package’s `caplo.yaml`. | | `name` | Yes | The project name: a lowercase letter, then lowercase letters, digits and hyphens. Releases published from the project record it. | | `agents` | No | Glob for agent files, relative to the project root. Default `agents/*.yaml`. | | `tools` | No | Glob for tool package directories, relative to the project root. Default `tools/*`. | | `environments` | No | Named workspaces the project publishes to. See [Environments](/cli/environments/). | Unknown fields are an error. Globs must stay inside the project, and they skip hidden files and folders such as `.DS_Store`. A directory the `tools` glob matches is a package only if it contains a `caplo.yaml`; others are ignored. ## Agent files [Section titled “Agent files”](#agent-files) Each agent is one YAML file in the agent YAML format the console exports, with `apiVersion: v2.0.0`. Files with `apiVersion` v1 are refused. See [Agent YAML](/agents/agent-yaml/) for how to write one and the [agent YAML reference](/reference/agent-yaml/) for every field. The file name without `.yaml` is the agent’s **key**, for example `orders` for `agents/orders.yaml`. Keys start with a lowercase letter and use only lowercase letters, digits and hyphens. Other agents in the project link to an agent by its key, and selectors can use it, so renaming the file is a breaking change inside the project. The agent’s `name` field is what customers and the console see. Inside a project, agent files refer to other resources like this: | In the agent file | Refers to | | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `subAgents: [{local: orders}]` | The project agent with key `orders` | | `subAgents: [{agentId: <ID>}]` | Any agent already in the workspace | | `tools: [order-desk/lookup_order]` | Tool `lookup_order` of the project package named `order-desk`; `order-desk` alone selects all its tools | | `tools: [crm/find_contact]` | A published tool of the workspace’s remote MCP server named `crm`, when no project package has that name | | `tools: [<PACKAGE_ID>/<TOOL>]` | A tool of any workspace package, by package ID | | `knowledgeBases: [Returns policy]` | The workspace knowledge base with that name, or an ID | A package’s name is the `name` in its own `caplo.yaml`, not its folder name. Names of remote MCP servers and knowledge bases are looked up in the workspace a command acts on, so the same file works in several workspaces. A sub-agent link with `release: latest`, or with no `release`, follows the child’s newest release; see [Sub-agents and routing](/agents/sub-agents-and-routing/). ## How files map to the workspace [Section titled “How files map to the workspace”](#how-files-map-to-the-workspace) The CLI matches project files to workspace resources **by name**: agents by their `name` field and packages by their package name. It never stores workspace IDs in your files. * A name the workspace does not have yet is created on the next push or publish. * A name it has is updated. * Nothing is ever deleted. Renaming an agent in its file therefore creates a new agent and leaves the old one in place. After a publish, `caplo publish --prune` archives agents that an earlier publish of this project created and that the project no longer defines. * If the workspace agent with that name is archived, the command stops. Restore the agent in the console, or rename it in the file. Agent names must be unique in the project, ignoring case. ## Select part of a project [Section titled “Select part of a project”](#select-part-of-a-project) Without arguments, `diff`, `push`, `test`, `eval` and `publish` act on the whole project. Pass selectors to act on part of it: 
```sh
caplo push agent/orders
caplo test tool/order-desk
caplo publish "agent/Shop Concierge" tool/store-stock
```
 | Selector | Selects | | --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `agent/<KEY_OR_NAME>` | One agent, by file key or by its `name`; a key wins over a name. It brings its project sub-agents and the project packages they use. | | `tool/<PACKAGE>` | One project package, by package name. | `caplo publish` also brings the project agents that link a selected agent at `release: latest`, because publishing the child publishes them again. A selector that matches nothing is a usage error. ## Run commands from anywhere in the project [Section titled “Run commands from anywhere in the project”](#run-commands-from-anywhere-in-the-project) Project commands look for the project file in the current directory, then in each parent directory, skipping tool packages’ own `caplo.yaml` files. So you can run them from inside `agents/` or a package folder. To point at a project elsewhere, pass `-C` (`--dir`): 
```sh
caplo validate -C ~/src/shop
```
 ## Check a project offline [Section titled “Check a project offline”](#check-a-project-offline) 
```sh
caplo validate
```
 `caplo validate` checks the project without calling the workspace: * `caplo.yaml` and every package’s `caplo.yaml` * every agent file against the agent schema, and unique file keys and agent names * references to project agents and packages, and the tools an `mcp` package declares * sub-agent link cycles, and each agent’s depth limit: at most `policy.maxDepth` (1 to 4, default 3) links below it * fast routing: at least one sub-agent and `doNotUseWhen` on every link, and, when the file has evals, two cases with `routedBy` per sub-agent * every [environment](/cli/environments/) in `caplo.yaml`, with its agent fields applied Problems are printed with their file and the command exits with code 3. Tools of remote MCP servers are checked against the workspace on `diff`, `push` and `publish`. `diff`, `push`, `test`, `eval` and `publish` run the same checks first and stop on any problem. ## Pull an existing agent [Section titled “Pull an existing agent”](#pull-an-existing-agent) To bring an agent you built in the console under version control: 
```sh
caplo pull "agent/Shop Concierge"
caplo pull agent/concierge ./shop
```
 `caplo pull` writes the agent, every sub-agent it links, and their eval cases as agent files. It also pulls the source of any package they use that the project lacks into the `tools` directory. Agents the project already defines are written to their existing files; new ones get a file named after the agent. The target is the project at the directory you give, or the project above `-C`. If there is none, the CLI creates a new project there, but never inside another project. Links name the exact release the draft pins, because a draft does not record `release: latest`; change them back to `release: latest` if you want parents to follow new child releases. Tools of remote MCP servers stay as `<server>/<tool>`. Caution `caplo pull` overwrites the agent files it writes. Commit or stash local changes first so you can review the difference. Next, see the [day-to-day workflow](/cli/workflow/).

# Script the CLI

> Machine-readable output, exit codes, environment variables, non-interactive use and shell completion for the caplo CLI.

Everything you need to call `caplo` from scripts, Makefiles and pipelines. For a ready-made pipeline, see [CI/CD](/cli/ci-cd/). ## Output formats [Section titled “Output formats”](#output-formats) Commands that show data take `--output` (`-o`) with `table` (the default), `json` or `yaml`: 
```sh
caplo agent list -o json
caplo workspace get -o yaml
caplo version -o json
```
 This covers the `list` and `get` commands, `caplo auth status`, `caplo config list`, `caplo version`, and `caplo workspace api-client` `list`, `create` and `rotate`. With `json` or `yaml`, standard output holds only that document, so you can pipe it straight into `jq`; prompts, warnings and errors go to standard error. Other commands print plain text meant for people. `caplo config get <key>` prints just the value: 
```sh
API_URL=$(caplo config get api-url)
```
 ### Plans as JSON [Section titled “Plans as JSON”](#plans-as-json) `caplo diff`, `caplo push --dry-run` and `caplo publish --dry-run` print their plan as one JSON or YAML document with `-o json` or `-o yaml`. `push` and `publish` accept `-o` only together with `--dry-run`, so the output stays a single document; without it they stop with exit code 2. | Field | Description | | -------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `project` | The project name | | `workspace` | `id` and `name` of the workspace; with an API key the name is the ID | | `environment` | The `--env` environment, if any | | `changes` | `true` if any resource has a step | | `resources` | One entry per package, agent and channel, in the order the command acts on them | | `firstPublish` | Publish only: `true` on the project’s first publish to this workspace | | `promotions` | Publish only, with `promoteFrom`: per agent, the environment it promotes `from`, the `source` digest and the matching `release` | Each entry in `resources` has: | Field | Description | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `resource` | `tool/<package>`, `agent/<key>` or `channel/<kind>/<name>` | | `steps` | What the command does, in order, from `create`, `update`, `save-evals`, `build`, `test`, `evaluate`, `publish`, `archive` and `deploy`; empty when unchanged | | `summary` | The line the table shows | | `files` | A package’s changed files, prefixed `+` added, `-` removed, `~` changed | | `reasons` | Why an agent publishes or is evaluated | | `current`, `next` | The current release or package version, and the release a publish makes | | `diff` | A unified diff of the agent’s draft and eval cases | For example, to run a step only when a publish would change something: 
```sh
if caplo publish --dry-run -o json | jq -e '.changes' > /dev/null; then
  echo "Publishing"
fi
```
 ## Exit codes [Section titled “Exit codes”](#exit-codes) | Code | Meaning | | ----- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `0` | Success. | | `1` | An error: the API refused the request, a file could not be read, a build failed, or the command failed in another way. | | `2` | A usage error: an unknown command or flag, a wrong argument, a confirmation that needs `--yes` because there is no terminal, or conflicting settings such as `--workspace` disagreeing with `--env`. | | `3` | A failed check: project validation, tool fixtures, eval cases, a promotion with no matching release, or a failed `caplo identity verify-assertion` check. | | `130` | Interrupted with Ctrl-C. A second Ctrl-C ends the process at once. | | `143` | Ended with SIGTERM. | Errors are printed to standard error as one line starting with `error:`. For a failed validation, each problem is printed above it with its file. ## Environment variables [Section titled “Environment variables”](#environment-variables) | Variable | Effect | | ------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `CAPLO_API_KEY` | Authenticate as an API client with this key instead of your login. See [API keys](/cli/authentication/#api-keys-for-automation). | | `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` | The workspace for every command, by ID or name, unless `--workspace` is given. With an API key it must be the ID. | | `CAPLO_ENV` | The project [environment](/cli/environments/) for `validate`, `diff`, `push`, `eval` and `publish`, unless `--env` is given. | | `CAPLO_API_URL` | The API that `caplo auth login` saves, unless `--api-url` is given, and the API an API key calls. | | `CAPLO_ISSUER` | The sign-in server that `caplo auth login` uses, unless `--issuer` is given. | | `CAPLO_CONFIG_DIR` | The configuration directory, instead of the default below. Use a separate one per Caplo login. | | `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` | On macOS and Linux, the configuration directory is `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/caplo` when `CAPLO_CONFIG_DIR` is not set; otherwise `~/.config/caplo`. On Windows it is `%AppData%\caplo`. | An environment’s `apiKeyEnv` in `caplo.yaml` names one more variable per environment, such as `CAPLO_PROD_KEY`; see [Environments](/cli/environments/#define-environments). ## Run without a terminal [Section titled “Run without a terminal”](#run-without-a-terminal) Commands that change something important ask first: `caplo publish`, `caplo agent publish`, `caplo channel deploy` and `rollback`, `caplo knowledge sync` when it removes documents, release and version revokes, archive, delete and remove commands, and secret unset, unbind and delete. Pass `--yes` (`-y`) to skip the question. When standard input is not a terminal, these commands do not read an answer from it. Without `--yes` they stop with exit code 2, so piping `y` into them does not work. `caplo publish` also requires `--yes` on a project’s first publish to a workspace, even in a terminal. ### Secrets from standard input [Section titled “Secrets from standard input”](#secrets-from-standard-input) Commands that take a secret never accept it as an argument, so it does not end up in your shell history or process list. They read it from a hidden prompt in a terminal, or from standard input otherwise: 
```sh
printf %s "$ORDERS_API_KEY" | caplo secret set orders-api-key
printf %s "$ORDERS_API_KEY" | caplo tool secret set ORDERS_API_KEY -p order-desk
```
 One trailing newline is removed; `printf %s` avoids adding one in the first place. Values are 1 to 8192 bytes. Remote MCP server credentials come from standard input or from a named environment variable, for example `caplo mcp add` with `--secret-stdin` or `--secret-env <VARIABLE>`. See [Secrets and egress](/tools/secrets-and-egress/) and [Remote MCP servers](/tools/remote-mcp/). API keys work the other way round: `caplo workspace api-client create` and `rotate` print the new key on standard output once, so you can pipe it into a secret store. With `-o json`, the key is in the `secret` field. ## Shell completion [Section titled “Shell completion”](#shell-completion) `caplo completion` prints a completion script for `bash`, `zsh`, `fish` or `powershell`. To try it in the current shell: 
```sh
source <(caplo completion zsh)
```
 To load it in every new zsh session, add that line to `~/.zshrc`. For bash, install the `bash-completion` package and save the script once: 
```sh
# macOS with Homebrew
dir="$(brew --prefix)/etc/bash_completion.d"
caplo completion bash > "$dir/caplo"


# Linux
caplo completion bash | sudo tee /etc/bash_completion.d/caplo > /dev/null
```
 For fish, run `caplo completion fish > ~/.config/fish/completions/caplo.fish`. For PowerShell, add `caplo completion powershell | Out-String | Invoke-Expression` to your profile. Homebrew installs do not set up completion for you. The [CLI reference](/reference/cli/#caplo-completion) lists the options.

# Build, test and publish a project

> The everyday caplo loop for a project, from validating files to publishing releases bottom up, and what each step changes in the workspace.

This page walks through the loop you repeat while building a [project](/cli/projects/). Every command below acts on the whole project unless you pass [selectors](/cli/projects/#select-part-of-a-project) such as `agent/orders` or `tool/order-desk`, and on the [selected workspace](/cli/workspaces/) unless you pass [`--env`](/cli/environments/). ## The loop at a glance [Section titled “The loop at a glance”](#the-loop-at-a-glance) 1. **Edit** agent files and tool code in your editor. 2. **Validate** offline with `caplo validate`. 3. **Test tools** locally with `caplo test`. 4. **Review** the difference from the workspace with `caplo diff`. 5. **Push** drafts with `caplo push`, then try the agent in a test chat. 6. **Evaluate** the drafts with `caplo eval`. 7. **Publish** releases with `caplo publish`. 8. **Pull** changes someone made in the console with `caplo pull`, when needed. Steps 2 to 4 change nothing in the workspace. Steps 5 and 6 change only drafts, which customers never see. Only step 7 creates releases. ## Validate [Section titled “Validate”](#validate) 
```sh
caplo validate
```
 Checks schemas, references, link cycles, the sub-agent depth limit, fast routing rules and every environment, without network access. It exits with code 3 and lists each problem with its file. The other project commands run the same checks first. See [Check a project offline](/cli/projects/#check-a-project-offline). ## Test tool packages [Section titled “Test tool packages”](#test-tool-packages) 
```sh
caplo test
caplo test tool/order-desk -v
```
 Runs each selected package’s fixtures on your machine, the same way as `caplo tool test`: it bundles the package like the Caplo builder and runs the fixtures in Node.js 24. It calls no workspace. Run `npm install` in each package first. A failing fixture exits with code 3; `-v` also prints log lines for passing fixtures. See [Test tools](/tools/testing/). ## Review the difference [Section titled “Review the difference”](#review-the-difference) 
```sh
caplo diff
caplo diff agent/concierge -o json
```
 Compares each package’s files with its workspace draft, and each agent file and its eval cases with the agent’s draft and eval suite. It prints a plan, one line per resource, and a unified diff for each changed agent. A new agent is shown against nothing. With `-o json` or `-o yaml`, it prints the plan as one document that a pipeline can post on a pull request; see [Scripting](/cli/scripting/#plans-as-json). ## Push drafts [Section titled “Push drafts”](#push-drafts) 
```sh
caplo push
caplo push --dry-run
```
 `caplo push` prints the plan, then: 1. Uploads each changed package as its workspace draft and builds it, streaming the build log. 2. Saves each agent file as its agent’s draft, children before parents, creating any agent the workspace does not have. 3. Saves each agent’s `evals` as its eval suite. An agent file without an `evals` key leaves the suite alone. Nothing is published. `--dry-run` prints the plan and diffs and stops. If nothing changed, push says so and does nothing. Caution A push replaces the workspace draft with your file. Edits someone made to that draft in the console since your last push are overwritten. Pull them first with `caplo pull` if you want to keep them. ### Try it in a test chat [Section titled “Try it in a test chat”](#try-it-in-a-test-chat) 
```sh
caplo agent chat "Shop Concierge"
```
 This opens a private test conversation with the agent’s draft in your terminal; you can also use the console’s test chat. A test conversation runs linked sub-agents from their **releases**, not their drafts, so a sub-agent you have never published cannot answer yet. Push tells you when that is the case and names the `caplo publish agent/<key>` that releases it. See [Test an agent](/agents/testing/). ## Evaluate [Section titled “Evaluate”](#evaluate) 
```sh
caplo eval
caplo eval agent/concierge
```
 Pushes the selection like `caplo push`, then runs each selected agent’s eval cases against its saved draft in isolated test conversations and prints the result of every case. A grader model also judges each case’s `desiredOutcome`; a failed case prints its rationale, and a case it could not grade counts as failed. If a passing run already exists for the same draft and eval cases, it is reused instead of run again. It exits with code 3 when any case fails or a selected agent has no eval cases. See [Evals](/agents/evals/) for how to write cases. ## Publish [Section titled “Publish”](#publish) 
```sh
caplo publish
caplo publish agent/orders --dry-run
caplo publish --yes
```
 `caplo publish` validates the project, works out what needs releasing, prints the plan with diffs, and asks `Publish to workspace <name>? [y/N]`. Then it publishes **bottom up**: 1. **Tool packages.** Each package that changed or has an unpublished build is pushed, built, tested with its fixtures in the workspace’s test runner, and published as a new version. 2. **Child agents.** Each sub-agent’s draft is saved, pinned to the package versions just published, its eval cases run, and it is published as a release. 3. **Parent agents.** Each parent’s draft is saved pinned to its children’s new releases, evaluated and published, until the top of the project. Every agent it publishes needs at least one eval case, and every case must pass. If a step fails, publishing stops there; what was already published stays published. ### What gets a new release [Section titled “What gets a new release”](#what-gets-a-new-release) An agent is published when any of these is true: * its file or eval cases differ from the workspace * its saved draft has never been published * a project package it uses publishes a new version, or its packages have newer versions than its release * a project sub-agent it links at `release: latest` publishes a new release An agent whose eval cases alone changed since its release is evaluated against the new cases and **keeps** its release, because a release does not include eval cases. Everything else is left unchanged and shown as `unchanged` in the plan. The plan shows each new version. A first release is 1.0.0. After that, changing the model or provider, changing sub-agent links, or removing a tool or knowledge base is a major bump; adding one is a minor bump; anything else is a patch. A new child release forces a major bump of its parents. ### Confirming [Section titled “Confirming”](#confirming) | Situation | What happens | | --------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A terminal, after the project’s first publish to this workspace | The CLI asks; answer `y` to publish | | `--yes` (`-y`) | Publishes without asking | | No terminal, such as CI, without `--yes` | Stops with exit code 2 | | The project’s first publish to a workspace, without `--yes` | Stops with exit code 2, even in a terminal, so you review the full plan first | `--dry-run` prints the plan and stops. Add `-o json` to get the plan as a document. ### Clean up removed agents [Section titled “Clean up removed agents”](#clean-up-removed-agents) 
```sh
caplo publish --prune
```
 Agents are never deleted when you remove their file. `--prune` adds an `archive` step to the plan for each agent that an earlier publish of this project created and that the project no longer defines, and archives them after publishing. It works on the whole project only, so it takes no selectors. Archived agents can be restored in the console. ## Pull console changes [Section titled “Pull console changes”](#pull-console-changes) 
```sh
caplo pull "agent/Shop Concierge"
```
 Writes the agent’s current draft, its sub-agents and their eval cases back into the project files, so you can commit changes made in the console. See [Pull an existing agent](/cli/projects/#pull-an-existing-agent). ## Next [Section titled “Next”](#next) * Publish to staging and production from the same files: [Environments](/cli/environments/). * Run this loop in a pipeline: [CI/CD](/cli/ci-cd/). * Every flag of these commands: [project command reference](/reference/cli/project/).

# Choose a workspace

> List, create and select workspaces with the caplo CLI, and control which workspace each command acts on.

Every command that reads or changes agents, tools, channels or knowledge acts on one workspace. The CLI remembers a selected workspace, and you can override it per command or per shell. For what a workspace holds and what each role can do, see [Workspaces and roles](/concepts/workspaces-and-roles/). ## List and inspect [Section titled “List and inspect”](#list-and-inspect) 
```sh
caplo workspace list
caplo workspace get
caplo workspace get "Acme Support"
```
 `caplo workspace list` shows each workspace you belong to with your role, its status and its ID; the selected one is marked with `*`. `caplo workspace get` shows one workspace, by default the selected one. Both take `-o json` or `-o yaml`. ## Select a workspace [Section titled “Select a workspace”](#select-a-workspace) 
```sh
caplo workspace use "Acme Support"
```
 You can name a workspace by its exact name or its ID. If two of your workspaces share a name, use the ID. The selection is saved in your [CLI configuration](/cli/authentication/#cli-settings) and applies to every later command. You rarely need to select one by hand: `caplo auth login` keeps your previous selection if you still belong to it, and selects your only workspace if you belong to exactly one. Changing the `api-url` setting clears the selection. ## Create a workspace [Section titled “Create a workspace”](#create-a-workspace) 
```sh
caplo workspace create "Acme Staging"
```
 You become its Owner, and the CLI selects it. Invite teammates in the console under **Settings**, **Members**. ## Which workspace a command uses [Section titled “Which workspace a command uses”](#which-workspace-a-command-uses) For each command, the CLI takes the first of these that is set: 1. The `--workspace` (`-w`) flag, for one command: `caplo agent list -w "Acme Staging"`. 2. The `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` environment variable, for every command in that shell or CI job. 3. The workspace selected with `caplo workspace use`. Project commands that take `--env` use the [environment’s](/cli/environments/) workspace instead. If `--workspace` or `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` names a different workspace, the command stops with a usage error (exit code 2) rather than guess which one you meant. Unset `CAPLO_WORKSPACE`, or set it to exactly the value in `caplo.yaml`, when you use environments. ## With an API key [Section titled “With an API key”](#with-an-api-key) When `CAPLO_API_KEY` is set, the workspace is always the key’s own workspace, and you must name it by ID with `--workspace`, `CAPLO_WORKSPACE` or an environment. A name is refused because a key cannot list workspaces. The `caplo workspace` commands themselves need a person’s login. See [API keys for automation](/cli/authentication/#api-keys-for-automation). API clients are managed under `caplo workspace api-client`; that is covered on the [authentication page](/cli/authentication/#create-a-client).

# Customer identity

> Verify who your customers are in the website widget, so tools can act on their own accounts and only verified customers see private content.

An agent on your website talks to anonymous visitors. Before a tool shows someone their orders or changes their address, Caplo must know who that person is, and the answer must not come from the model or the chat: a visitor can type any name or account number. **Customer identity** lets visitors prove who they are through a sign-in you control. Caplo records the result as a verified binding for the conversation, and tools receive the verified customer from Caplo, never from the model. Verification is available in the [website widget](/channels/website-widget/install/), in text and voice. WhatsApp, Messenger, Instagram and phone conversations are always anonymous. ## What verification unlocks [Section titled “What verification unlocks”](#what-verification-unlocks) * **Customer-aware tools** act for the verified customer and can send your API a signed customer context token. See [Build customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/). * **Knowledge for verified customers only**: knowledge bases with the audience **Verified customers only** answer only after the visitor verifies. See [Knowledge](/agents/knowledge/). * **Previous conversation**: the channel can show a verified customer their most recent earlier conversation. ## Connection types [Section titled “Connection types”](#connection-types) You set up the ways customers can verify as **connections** in the console under **Settings** then **Customer identity**. Each connection says how a customer proves who they are and which ID in your system they become (the `sub`). Managing customer identity needs the Owner or Admin role. | Connection type | How the customer verifies | Use it when | | --------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Host sign-in** | Your website’s backend signs a short-lived assertion for the person already signed in on your site. The widget gets it from your page; the visitor does nothing. | Customers use the widget on pages where they are already signed in. This is the strongest and smoothest option. See [Host sign-in with signed assertions](/identity/signed-assertions/). | | **Provider sign-in** | The visitor signs in with your OpenID Connect provider in a popup window. | Your customers have accounts at an OpenID Connect provider, and the widget runs on pages where they are not signed in. See [Provider sign-in with OpenID Connect](/identity/oidc/). | | **Verification code** | The visitor enters their email, phone number or account number. Caplo asks your webhook to send a one-time code to the contact details you have on file. | You have no customer sign-in, or need a fallback. Codes always verify at the lowest level. See [Verification codes](/identity/verification-codes/). | Connections that share a **Subject namespace** identify the same customers, so a customer who verifies with a code and later with host sign-in is the same person to Caplo. The namespace defaults to the connection’s issuer (a verification code connection gets a namespace of its own) and cannot change after the connection exists, so set it when you create the connection if several connections should share customers. ## Assurance levels [Section titled “Assurance levels”](#assurance-levels) Each verification has a level that says how strongly the customer proved who they are. | Level | Typical source | | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `low` | A verification code. A sign-in your mapping rates low. | | `standard` | A normal password sign-in. Host and provider sign-ins without an `acr` claim get this level by default. | | `high` | A sign-in with multi-factor authentication. A `high` requirement also needs the customer to have authenticated within the last 15 minutes, unless you set a freshness. | For host and provider sign-in, the level comes from the `acr` claim through the connection’s **Assurance mapping**: you list each `acr` value your sign-in sends and the level it means, and choose what happens **When acr is absent** (**Low**, **Standard** or **Reject the sign-in**). An `acr` value you did not list is refused. Verification codes are always `low`. When an operation needs a higher level or a more recent sign-in than the customer has, the widget asks them to **Confirm it’s you**, and the operation waits until they do. This is called step-up. ## The verification policy [Section titled “The verification policy”](#the-verification-policy) The **Verification policy** panel on the same page decides whether visitors can verify at all and what a verification grants: * **Verify customers in support conversations** must be on. * **Connections, in order**: up to 8 connections customers may use. A connection must be listed here and on the channel to be offered. * **Allowed scopes**: what a verification may grant. `identity:bind` is required. * **Minimum level per scope**: the level each scope needs. Without a row, `identity:bind`, `knowledge:verified` and tool reads need `low`; tool writes and memory need `standard`. * **Session timeouts**: how long a verification lasts at each level, idle and absolute. The defaults are 30 minutes idle and 4 hours absolute for `low`, and 60 minutes idle and 12 hours absolute for `standard` and `high`. | Scope | Grants | | --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | | `identity:bind` | Verifying at all. | | `knowledge:verified` | Knowledge bases for verified customers only. | | `memory:read`, `memory:write` | Long-term customer memory, for agents that use it. | | `tools:read:<operation>`, `tools:write:<operation>` | One verified tool operation on a channel. | Caution Saving the policy ends every current verification: verified visitors must verify again. Change it outside busy hours. With **Require a second approver** on (under **Trust changes**), creating a connection, enabling it, or changing its issuer, keys, endpoints, webhook, mappings, attributes or API audiences waits as a pending trust change until another Owner or Admin approves it. Disabling a connection and revoking a key always apply at once. Trust changes are recorded in the [audit log](/operate/audit-log/); nobody is notified, so tell the approver. ## How verification works in the widget [Section titled “How verification works in the widget”](#how-verification-works-in-the-widget) Each website channel decides who may verify. In the console, open the channel, select **Configure & install**, and go to the **Conversation** tab. Under **Customer verification**, choose **Anonymous visitors only**, **Visitors may verify who they are** or **Visitors must verify before chatting**, then select **Choose sign-in methods and consent for this channel** to pick the connections and consent. See [Website widget settings](/channels/website-widget/settings/#customer-verification). 1. The widget offers the channel’s provider sign-in and verification code methods in order, as buttons labelled with the button label you set on the channel or, without one, the connection’s name. 2. With the consent mode **Ask for agreement before the first verification**, the visitor agrees to your consent text first. With **Show a notice after verification**, they verify straight away and then see a notice, such as “Signed in. This chat can see your account.”, with **Not you? Sign out**. 3. Host sign-in is never a button. It runs on its own when your page has registered a token provider and the consent mode is **Show a notice after verification**. 4. Once verified, the widget shows who is signed in, and tools, knowledge and history unlock as the policy allows. 5. Visitors can **Sign out** at any time. You can also revoke verifications from the console or from your own systems. See [Account links and revocation](/identity/account-links-and-revocation/). ## How the customer reaches your tools [Section titled “How the customer reaches your tools”](#how-the-customer-reaches-your-tools) A tool that declares `subject` in `caplo.yaml` receives the verified customer in `ctx.subject`: `externalSubject` is the `sub` your sign-in asserted, and `level` is the assurance level. For a stronger guarantee, Caplo can also give the tool a **customer context token**: a 60-second JWT, signed by your workspace’s issuer, that your own API verifies before acting. See [Build customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/) and [Verify customers in your API](/identity/verify-in-your-api/). Caution A customer-aware tool runs only when its operation is listed in the channel’s **Verified tool operations**, with a scope such as `tools:read:list_my_orders` that the policy allows. The console shows those operations and lets you change their level, freshness and token audience, but adding an operation to a channel is not yet available in the console or the CLI. Contact [support](/resources/support/) with the channel and the tools to set it up. ## Setup checklist [Section titled “Setup checklist”](#setup-checklist) 1. Choose a connection type and create the connection in **Settings**, **Customer identity**: [host sign-in](/identity/signed-assertions/), [provider sign-in](/identity/oidc/) or [verification codes](/identity/verification-codes/). 2. Set the **Assurance mapping** so each `acr` value your sign-in sends gives the right level. 3. In **Verification policy**, turn verification on, add the connection, allow `identity:bind` and the scopes you need, and save. 4. On the website channel, allow verification, choose the sign-in methods and consent mode, and select **Save entry point**. 5. For host sign-in, add the token endpoint to your backend and call `identity.configure` on your page. See the [JavaScript API](/channels/website-widget/javascript-api/#sign-visitors-in-from-your-page). 6. For tools that act on the customer’s account, follow [Build customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/), and verify customer context tokens in your API. 7. Decide how sign-outs and revoked accounts reach Caplo: [Account links and revocation](/identity/account-links-and-revocation/). 8. Test the whole flow on a test channel, and watch **Activity** on the **Customer identity** page for failed verifications and their reasons. [Host sign-in](/identity/signed-assertions/)Sign visitors in from your own backend with a short-lived assertion. [Provider sign-in](/identity/oidc/)Let visitors sign in with your OpenID Connect provider. [Verification codes](/identity/verification-codes/)Send one-time codes through your own webhook. [Verify in your API](/identity/verify-in-your-api/)Check Caplo's customer context token before acting.

# Account links and revocation

> End customer verifications from the console or from your own identity systems, handle sign-out in the widget, and rename or merge customer IDs.

A verification lasts until the visitor signs out, the session timeout for its level runs out, or something ends it early. This page covers the ways to end it early: from the console, from your identity provider or backend with back-channel logout and Shared Signals, and from the widget. It also covers renaming and merging customer IDs, and account links. Revoking a verification never deletes a conversation. The visitor loses verified access at their next turn and is asked to verify again. ## Revoke from the console [Section titled “Revoke from the console”](#revoke-from-the-console) On **Settings**, **Customer identity** (Owner or Admin): * **Revoke bindings**, on the **Activity** panel, opens a dialog where you choose what to revoke, the **Connection** and a **Reason**: * **Every binding of one customer**: enter the **Customer subject (sub)**. * **Every binding verified under a key**: enter the **Key ID (kid)**. * **Every binding of a connection**. * **Revoke subject**, next to a binding under **Recent bindings**, revokes that customer. * **Disable** on a connection stops new verifications through it and revokes every binding made through it. **Enable** brings it back, as a trust change; earlier visitors must verify again. * **Revoke key** on a host sign-in connection refuses that key from now on and revokes the bindings verified with it. See [Rotate keys](/identity/signed-assertions/#rotate-keys). Revocations take effect at once, even when **Require a second approver** is on, and are recorded in the [audit log](/operate/audit-log/). ## Back-channel logout [Section titled “Back-channel logout”](#back-channel-logout) When a customer signs out of your site or your provider, you can end their verification in Caplo too, with an OpenID Connect back-channel logout token. This works for host sign-in and provider sign-in connections. 1. On the connection, turn on **Back-channel logout** and save. Approve the trust change if your workspace requires a second approver. 2. Open the connection again with **Configure** and copy the **Back-channel logout URL**: `https://api.caplo.si/v1/identity/customer-signals/<CONNECTION_ID>/backchannel-logout`. 3. Register it as the client’s back-channel logout URL at your provider, or have your backend call it when a session ends. Caplo expects a form `POST` with one field, `logout_token`, holding a JWT that: | Part | Rule | | ------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Header `typ` | `logout+jwt`. | | Signature | A key Caplo already trusts for the connection: your host sign-in keys (not revoked, with an allowed algorithm) or your provider’s JWKS. | | `iss` | The connection’s issuer, or the provider’s issuer. | | `aud` | For host sign-in, the connection’s audience (`urn:caplo:customer-identity:<CONNECTION_ID>`). For provider sign-in, the client ID. | | `iat`, `exp` | `iat` within the last 24 hours and not in the future; `exp` optional. | | `jti` | Required and unique. Each token works once. | | `events` | Contains `http://schemas.openid.net/event/backchannel-logout`. | | `sid`, `sub` | At least one. With `sid`, only verifications from that session end; it must match the `sid` of the assertion or ID token. With only `sub`, every verification of that customer through the connection ends. | | `nonce` | Must be absent. | Caplo answers `200` when the token is accepted, and `400` without detail when it is not. ## Shared Signals [Section titled “Shared Signals”](#shared-signals) If your identity provider or backend can transmit Shared Signals (OpenID SSF) events, Caplo can react to them as they happen. 1. On the connection, turn on **Shared Signals (SSF)** and save. 2. Open it again and copy the **SSF push URL** and the **SSF stream audience**. 3. Configure your transmitter to push (RFC 8935) to that URL, with that audience, signed with the same keys as above. Each Security Event Token is sent with `Content-Type: application/secevent+jwt`, has header `typ` `secevent+jwt`, `iss` the connection’s issuer, `aud` the stream audience, a unique `jti` and an `iat` within the last 24 hours, and up to 8 events. It must not have a top-level `sub`. Name the customer in `sub_id` or each event’s `subject`, in the format `iss_sub` (with `iss` the connection’s issuer and `sub` the customer’s ID) or `opaque` (with `id` the customer’s ID). | Event | What Caplo does | | ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | CAEP `session-revoked` | Revokes the customer’s verifications through the connection. | | RISC `account-disabled` | Revokes the customer’s verifications through the connection. | | CAEP `credential-change` | Revokes verifications authenticated before the event’s `event_timestamp`. | | CAEP `assurance-level-change` | Lowers the level of current verifications to the level `current_level` maps to through the connection’s **Assurance mapping**. It never raises a level, and a value you did not map revokes. | | RISC `identifier-changed` | Proposes renaming the customer to `new-value`. The proposal waits under **Subject changes** until an Owner or Admin selects **Confirm**. | Other event types are accepted and ignored. Caplo answers `202` when the token is accepted, and `400` without detail when it is not. Every signal is recorded in the audit log. ## Sign-out in the widget [Section titled “Sign-out in the widget”](#sign-out-in-the-widget) Visitors can end their own verification: * **Sign out** in the widget, or **Not you? Sign out** on the notice shown after verifying. * **New conversation**, which signs them out first. When the visitor was signed in by your page with host sign-in, the widget does not sign them out on its own. It dispatches `caplo:identity-signout-requested` on the widget control, so your page can sign the user out of your site and then call `identity.signOut()`. Call `identity.signOut()` too whenever your user signs out on your site. See the [JavaScript API](/channels/website-widget/javascript-api/#sign-visitors-in-from-your-page). ## Rename or merge customers [Section titled “Rename or merge customers”](#rename-or-merge-customers) When a customer’s ID changes in your system, or two IDs turn out to be the same person, select **Rename or merge** under **Subject changes**: * **Rename: the customer’s ID changed** moves the customer from **Current subject** to **New subject**. * **Merge: two IDs are the same customer** folds the **Subject to merge away** into the **Subject to keep**. Choose the **Subject namespace** and give a **Reason**. Memory, account links and conversation history move with the customer, the old ID’s active sessions end, and the change is audited. Use this instead of sending a new `sub` from your sign-in: a new `sub` is a new customer to Caplo. ## Account links [Section titled “Account links”](#account-links) An account link maps a verified customer to an account in one of your backend systems, named by a namespace such as `billing`, and can hold a credential for that account. A verified tool operation that names the namespace then acts on the linked account, and Caplo passes the credential to the tool as one of its declared package secrets, only for that customer and only for that call. The value is never shown again after it is stored, and revoking a link destroys it. If a customer has no active link, the operation is refused, but they stay verified. Account links work together with verified tool operations on a channel. Neither can be set up from the console or the CLI yet; contact [support](/resources/support/) if you need them.

# Provider sign-in with OpenID Connect

> Let website visitors verify by signing in with your OpenID Connect provider in a popup window.

With **Provider sign-in**, the visitor signs in with your own OpenID Connect provider, such as the one your customer app uses. The widget opens the provider’s sign-in page in a separate window; Caplo runs the authorization code flow with PKCE, checks the ID token, and verifies the visitor as the provider’s `sub`. Use it when visitors are not already signed in on the pages that show the widget. If they are, [host sign-in](/identity/signed-assertions/) is smoother. ## What your provider needs [Section titled “What your provider needs”](#what-your-provider-needs) * The authorization code flow with PKCE (`S256`), returning the code in the query string. * The `iss` parameter in the authorization response (RFC 9207). Caplo refuses a callback without it. * ID tokens signed with `RS256`, `PS256` or `ES256`, with a `kid` in the header, from keys published at the provider’s JWKS URL, and valid for at most an hour. * A client registered for Caplo. Use a public client that needs no client secret. See [Client authentication](#client-authentication). Caplo calls your provider’s discovery, token and JWKS endpoints from the internet over HTTPS, without following redirects, so they must be publicly reachable. ## Set it up [Section titled “Set it up”](#set-it-up) 1. **Register a client at your provider** with: * Redirect URI: `https://api.caplo.si/v1/messaging/customer-login/callback`. The connection editor shows the same value as **Callback URL to register**; register it exactly. * Grant type: authorization code, with PKCE required. * Client type: public (no secret). * The scopes Caplo will ask for: `openid`, plus `profile` or `email` if you want the visitor’s name or email address. 2. **Create the connection.** In the console, go to **Settings**, **Customer identity**, select **Add connection**, and choose **Provider sign-in**: | Field | What to enter | | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Connection name** | Shown on the widget’s sign-in button unless the channel sets a button label. | | **Subject namespace** | Optional; defaults to the provider’s issuer. Fixed once created. | | **Provider configuration** | **Discovery URL**, such as `https://login.example.com/.well-known/openid-configuration`. Caplo reads the issuer and endpoints from it when you save. Or **Enter endpoints** for the issuer, authorization, token and JWKS URLs. | | **Client ID** | The client you registered. | | **Client authentication** | **None (public client with PKCE)**. | | **Requested scopes** | Space-separated, including `openid`. | | **Authorization details type** | Optional. When set, a step-up for a specific action sends an RFC 9396 `authorization_details` entry of this type with the action’s summary, for providers that show it. | | **Assurance mapping** | Each `acr` value your provider returns and its level, and the level **When acr is absent**. See [Assurance levels](/identity/#assurance-levels). | | **Accepted profile claims** | Which of `name`, `email`, `email_verified` and `locale` Caplo keeps from the ID token. Others are ignored. | Select **Create connection**. 3. **Add it to the policy.** In **Verification policy**, turn on **Verify customers in support conversations**, add the connection, make sure **Allowed scopes** includes `identity:bind`, and select **Save policy**. **Provider renewal period** sets how often Caplo renews a provider sign-in on its own, from 30 to 300 seconds. 4. **Turn it on for the channel.** On the website channel’s **Conversation** tab, allow verification under **Customer verification**, select **Choose sign-in methods and consent for this channel**, add the connection as a sign-in method with an optional button label, and select **Save entry point**. See [Website widget settings](/channels/website-widget/settings/#customer-verification). 5. **Test it** on a test channel: open the widget, sign in, and check **Activity** on the **Customer identity** page. Provider sign-in connections have no **Test assertion** action. ## Claims mapping [Section titled “Claims mapping”](#claims-mapping) | ID token claim | How Caplo uses it | | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `sub` | Becomes the customer’s ID, the `externalSubject` and `sub` your tools and API receive. It must be stable. | | `acr` | Mapped to a level. An `acr` value that is not in the mapping refuses the sign-in. | | `auth_time` | When the customer authenticated. Required when Caplo asks for a recent sign-in (`max_age`). | | `sid` | Lets your provider’s [back-channel logout](/identity/account-links-and-revocation/#back-channel-logout) end this sign-in. | | `amr` | Kept with the verification, up to 16 values. | | `name`, `email`, `email_verified`, `locale` | Kept only when listed under **Accepted profile claims**. The widget shows `name` as “Signed in as”. | The ID token’s `aud` must be your client ID, and its `nonce` must match the one Caplo sent. The provider’s access and refresh tokens are not given to tools or agents. ## Step-up [Section titled “Step-up”](#step-up) When an operation needs a higher level or a more recent sign-in, Caplo starts a new sign-in with `acr_values` set to the mapped `acr` values that meet the level, and `max_age` set to the freshness, if any. Without a requirement, Caplo sends neither and no `prompt`, so a visitor already signed in at your provider is not asked again. Make sure your provider honors `acr_values` and `max_age` for the levels you map, and returns `acr` and `auth_time` in the ID token. ## What customers see [Section titled “What customers see”](#what-customers-see) 1. The widget shows a button with the connection name or the channel’s button label. 2. With the consent mode **Ask for agreement before the first verification**, the visitor reads your consent text and ticks **I agree to the terms above.** 3. Selecting the button opens your provider’s sign-in page in a separate window (“Sign-in opens in a separate secure window.”). If the browser blocks the window, the widget offers **Continue sign-in**. 4. After the visitor signs in, the window closes and the widget shows them as signed in, by name when the `name` claim is accepted. 5. For a step-up, the widget shows **Confirm it’s you**, and the same window opens again. ## Client authentication [Section titled “Client authentication”](#client-authentication) Use **None (public client with PKCE)**. **Client secret (basic)** needs Caplo to store the client secret for the connection, which you cannot do yourself yet: contact [support](/resources/support/) if your provider requires a confidential client. **Private key JWT** is not supported for provider sign-in yet.

# Host sign-in with signed assertions

> Sign visitors who are already signed in on your site in to the website widget, with a short-lived assertion your backend signs.

With **Host sign-in**, your website vouches for its own signed-in user. When the widget needs to know who the visitor is, it asks your page for a token; your page asks your backend, which checks its own session and signs a short **assertion** naming the customer. Caplo checks the signature against the public key you registered and verifies the visitor. The visitor clicks nothing, and Caplo never sees your passwords, cookies or private keys. In order: 1. The widget gets a one-time `nonce` from Caplo and calls your page’s `getToken` with it. 2. Your page posts the nonce to your backend. 3. Your backend checks its own session and returns an assertion for the signed-in customer, signed with your private key and valid for about a minute. 4. The widget sends the assertion to Caplo, which checks the signature, issuer, audience, nonce and times, and verifies the visitor as that customer. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * The Owner or Admin role, to manage **Customer identity**. * A website channel whose widget runs on pages where your customers are signed in. See [Install the website widget](/channels/website-widget/install/). * The [caplo CLI](/cli/install/), for generating keys and testing assertions. Nothing in `caplo identity` talks to your workspace. ## Set it up [Section titled “Set it up”](#set-it-up) 1. **Generate a signing key.** Run this where your backend’s secrets live: 
   ```sh
   caplo identity keygen --alg ES256 --kid 2026-11 --out ./caplo-keys
   ```
 It writes `2026-11.private.jwk.json` and `2026-11.private.pem` (readable only by you) and `2026-11.public.jwk.json`. The key ID defaults to the key’s thumbprint when you leave out `--kid`. Other algorithms are `ES384`, `PS256`, `RS256` and `EdDSA`. Store the private key in your backend’s secret store; it never goes to Caplo. 2. **Create the connection.** In the console, go to **Settings**, **Customer identity**, and select **Add connection**: | Field | What to enter | | ------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Connection type** | **Host sign-in**. | | **Connection name** | Up to 80 characters, shown in the console. | | **Subject namespace** | Optional. Leave it empty to use the issuer. Connections that share a namespace identify the same customers. Fixed once created. | | **Issuer (iss)** | The exact value your backend puts in `iss`, for example `https://www.example.com`. | | **Public keys** | **JWKS URL (recommended for rotation)**: an HTTPS URL that serves your public keys as a JWK Set. Or **Paste public JWKs (up to 4)**: the contents of `2026-11.public.jwk.json`. Every key needs `kid` and `alg`. | | **Allowed algorithms** | The algorithms your keys use. A key whose `alg` is not allowed is refused. | | **Assurance mapping** | Each `acr` value you send and its level, and the level **When acr is absent**. See [Assurance levels](/identity/#assurance-levels). | | **Claims and attributes** | The profile claims (`name`, `email`, `email_verified`, `locale`) and typed attributes you will send. Anything not listed is refused. | Select **Create connection**, then open it again with **Configure** and copy the **Audience (aud)**. It looks like `urn:caplo:customer-identity:<CONNECTION_ID>` and never changes. 3. **Add it to the policy.** In **Verification policy**, turn on **Verify customers in support conversations**, add the connection under **Connections, in order**, make sure **Allowed scopes** includes `identity:bind`, and select **Save policy**. 4. **Add a token endpoint to your backend.** See [Write the token endpoint](#write-the-token-endpoint). The connection editor also shows ready-made Node.js, Python and Go examples filled in with your issuer and audience. 5. **Register a token provider on your page** with the widget’s JavaScript API: 
   ```js
   window.CaploWidget.get('<WIDGET_ID>').identity.configure({
     when: 'open',
     getToken: async ({ nonce, requirement }) => {
       const response = await fetch('/support/caplo-token', {
         method: 'POST',
         headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json', 'x-caplo-token-request': '1' },
         body: JSON.stringify({ nonce, requirement }),
       });
       return response.status === 200 ? response.text() : null;
     },
   });
   ```
 Return `null` when nobody is signed in on your page. `when` decides when the widget asks; call `identity.refresh()` after your user signs in or switches account and `identity.signOut()` after they sign out. See [Sign visitors in from your page](/channels/website-widget/javascript-api/#sign-visitors-in-from-your-page). 6. **Turn it on for the channel.** Open the website channel, select **Configure & install**, and go to the **Conversation** tab. Set **Customer verification** to **Visitors may verify who they are** or **Visitors must verify before chatting**, select **Choose sign-in methods and consent for this channel**, add the connection as a sign-in method, set **Consent mode** to **Show a notice after verification**, write the **Consent text** (16 to 4096 characters), and select **Save entry point**. Caution Host sign-in runs only with the consent mode **Show a notice after verification**. With **Ask for agreement before the first verification**, or without channel sign-in settings at all, the widget never asks your page for a token, and a channel that requires verification leaves the visitor unable to chat. ## Write the token endpoint [Section titled “Write the token endpoint”](#write-the-token-endpoint) The endpoint is yours, on your own domain. It must: * Accept only `POST` requests from your own site, and check the `Origin` header. * Require a custom header, such as `x-caplo-token-request: 1`, so a form on another site cannot call it. * Identify the visitor from your own session cookie (`SameSite`), never from the request body. * Take the issuer and audience from server configuration, never from the request. * Sign only the `nonce` and `requirement` it is given. * Answer `Cache-Control: no-store`, and `401` when nobody is signed in. 
```js
// npm install jose
import { SignJWT, importPKCS8 } from "jose";


const ISSUER = "https://www.example.com";                       // the connection's Issuer (iss)
const AUDIENCE = "urn:caplo:customer-identity:<CONNECTION_ID>"; // the connection's Audience (aud)
const SITE_ORIGIN = "https://www.example.com";
const privateKey = await importPKCS8(process.env.CAPLO_SIGNING_KEY, "ES256"); // 2026-11.private.pem


// Express: app.post("/support/caplo-token", express.json(), caploToken)
export async function caploToken(req, res) {
  res.set("Cache-Control", "no-store");
  if (req.get("origin") !== SITE_ORIGIN || req.get("x-caplo-token-request") !== "1") return res.sendStatus(403);
  const user = req.session?.user;
  if (!user) return res.sendStatus(401);
  const { nonce, requirement } = req.body ?? {};
  if (typeof nonce !== "string") return res.sendStatus(400);
  if (requirement && !meets(user, requirement)) return res.sendStatus(401);
  const token = await new SignJWT({ nonce, acr: user.mfa ? "mfa" : "pwd", auth_time: user.authTime })
    .setProtectedHeader({ alg: "ES256", kid: "2026-11", typ: "caplo-customer+jwt" })
    .setIssuer(ISSUER)
    .setAudience(AUDIENCE)
    .setSubject(user.customerId) // stable and opaque, never an email or phone number
    .setIssuedAt()
    .setExpirationTime("60s")
    .sign(privateKey);
  res.type("text/plain").send(token);
}


// A step-up asks for a level, a recent sign-in, or both. Sign only when this session meets it.
function meets(user, { level, maxAuthAgeSeconds }) {
  if (level === "high" && !user.mfa) return false;
  if (maxAuthAgeSeconds !== undefined && Date.now() / 1000 - user.authTime > maxAuthAgeSeconds) return false;
  return true;
}
```
 This example assumes the connection maps `acr` `mfa` to **High** and `pwd` to **Standard**. ### Assertion format [Section titled “Assertion format”](#assertion-format) The assertion is a compact JWS of at most 16 KiB. Its header has `typ` `caplo-customer+jwt`, an `alg` that matches the key’s `alg` and is allowed on the connection, and a `kid` that names an unrevoked key. Headers `crit`, `jku`, `jwk`, `x5u`, `x5c` and `x5t` are refused. | Claim | Required | Rules | | ------------------------------------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `iss` | Yes | Exactly the connection’s issuer. | | `aud` | Yes | Exactly the connection’s audience, as a string or a one-value list. | | `sub` | Yes | Your stable, opaque customer ID, up to 255 characters. This is the ID tools receive. | | `nonce` | Yes | The `nonce` the widget passed to `getToken`, unchanged. Each nonce works once. | | `iat`, `exp` | Yes | `exp` at most 300 seconds after `iat`; 60 is recommended. Mint the assertion when asked: one issued before the widget’s request is refused. Caplo allows 60 seconds of clock skew. | | `nbf` | No | Not before; must not be in the future. | | `acr` | No | Mapped to a level through **Assurance mapping**. An unmapped value is refused. | | `auth_time` | No | When the customer last authenticated, in seconds. Needed to meet a freshness requirement. Must not be after `iat`. | | `amr` | No | Authentication methods, such as `["pwd","otp"]`, up to 16. | | `sid` | No | A non-secret ID of the customer’s session on your site, so a [back-channel logout](/identity/account-links-and-revocation/#back-channel-logout) can end it. | | `jti` | No | A unique ID, up to 255 characters. | | `name`, `email`, `email_verified`, `locale` | No | Only those allowed under **Accepted profile claims**. The widget shows `name` as “Signed in as”. | | `attributes` | No | An object of declared attributes, such as `{"tier":"gold"}`, checked against each attribute’s type. Up to 20. | | `act_for` | No | `{"sub": "<PRINCIPAL_ID>", "relationship": "caregiver"}` when a person acts for another customer. The relationship must be listed under **Delegation relationships**. Tools then act for the principal. | Any other claim is refused, so a mistake shows up as a failed verification in **Activity** instead of passing silently. ## Renewal and step-up [Section titled “Renewal and step-up”](#renewal-and-step-up) A host sign-in lasts 15 minutes and is renewed in the background: while the page is open, the widget calls `getToken` again about every 10 minutes, with a new nonce and `requirement` `null`. After a page reload, the widget hides the transcript until your page confirms the same person with a fresh token. If `getToken` returns `null`, the visitor is signed out. If it returns an assertion for a different `sub`, the conversation ends and the new person starts a fresh one. When an operation needs more than the current sign-in, the widget shows **Confirm it’s you**. When the visitor selects it, `getToken` receives a `requirement` with `level`, optionally `maxAuthAgeSeconds`, and for a specific action an `action` with its `id`, `summary` and `digest`. Your page can run its own multi-factor prompt before calling your backend: the widget waits up to 5 minutes. Sign only when the session meets the requirement, with an `acr` that maps to the level and an `auth_time` within `maxAuthAgeSeconds`, and for the same `sub`. ## Test it [Section titled “Test it”](#test-it) Before you put it on a live site, test your assertions with the CLI and in the console. 1. Sign an assertion locally, as your backend would: 
   ```sh
   caplo identity sign-assertion --key ./caplo-keys/2026-11.private.pem --kid 2026-11 \
     --iss https://www.example.com --aud urn:caplo:customer-identity:<CONNECTION_ID> \
     --sub cus_1234 --nonce test-nonce-0123456789 --acr mfa --auth-time now --ttl 60
   ```
 2. Check an assertion, from the CLI or from your own endpoint, against your public key: 
   ```sh
   caplo identity verify-assertion - --jwks ./caplo-keys/2026-11.public.jwk.json \
     --iss https://www.example.com --aud urn:caplo:customer-identity:<CONNECTION_ID> \
     --nonce test-nonce-0123456789 < assertion.txt
   ```
 It prints each check as `PASS`, `FAIL` or `SKIP` and exits with status 3 when one fails. `--jwks` also takes your HTTPS JWKS URL. The connection’s own rules (acr mapping, allowed claims, attributes) are skipped locally. 3. In the console, select **Test assertion** on the connection and paste an assertion your servers signed. Every check runs, including the connection’s rules, except the nonce, and nothing is created. 4. Open the widget on a test channel while signed in to your site. Failed attempts appear under **Activity**, **Failures by reason**. See the [`caplo identity` reference](/reference/cli/identity/) for every flag. ## Rotate keys [Section titled “Rotate keys”](#rotate-keys) With a **JWKS URL**, Caplo caches your key set for its `Cache-Control` max-age, between 5 minutes and 24 hours, and fetches it again (at most once a minute) when an assertion names a key ID it does not know. To rotate: 1. Generate a new key and publish its public JWK in your JWKS alongside the old one. 2. Start signing with the new key. 3. Remove the old key from your JWKS once nothing signs with it. Select **Flush keys** on the connection to make Caplo drop its cached copy at once. With pasted keys, add the new public JWK to the connection (up to 4), switch your backend to it, then remove the old one. Each edit is a trust change and may need a second approver. Caution If a private key may have leaked, select **Revoke key** on the connection. The key is refused from then on, whatever any cache holds, and every verification made with it is revoked. Then publish a new key. AI prompt Sign my signed-in users in to the Caplo widget Adds a token endpoint that signs Caplo host sign-in assertions, wires identity.configure on my pages, and tests it with the caplo CLI. Copy prompt[Open in
