# Caplo documentation

> Build AI agents that answer from your knowledge, act through your tools, and talk to customers on your website, in messaging apps and on the phone.

## Find your way [Section titled “Find your way”](#find-your-way) [Get started](/get-started/what-is-caplo/)What Caplo is, and your first agent in the console or with the CLI. [Concepts](/concepts/workspaces-and-roles/)Workspaces, agents and releases, tools, knowledge, channels, conversations and evals. [Build agents](/agents/instructions-and-model/)Instructions, models, knowledge, tools, sub-agents, testing, evals and publishing. [Tools](/tools/)Write tool packages, connect remote MCP servers, and let agents read and write safely. [Website widget](/channels/website-widget/install/)Install the chat widget on your site, design it, and control it from JavaScript. [Models and providers](/providers/)Choose models, bring your own provider keys, and set up voice. [Usage and billing](/usage/)Understand usage, read statements, and set spend budgets. [CLI](/cli/install/)Install the caplo CLI and manage projects, environments and CI/CD. [Reference](/reference/cli/)Every CLI command, the agent YAML schema, caplo.yaml and tool fixtures. ## Use the docs with AI [Section titled “Use the docs with AI”](#use-the-docs-with-ai) The docs are written to be read by coding agents and chat assistants as well as people. * Every page has a Markdown version. Replace the trailing slash of a page URL with `.md`: `/agents/publishing/` is `/agents/publishing.md`, and the home page is `/index.md`. * `https://docs.caplo.si/llms.txt` lists every page with a short description, and `https://docs.caplo.si/llms-full.txt` is the whole site as one Markdown file. * Task pages include an **AI prompt** you can copy, or open in Claude or ChatGPT, to have an assistant do the task in your own repository.

# Audit log

> What the workspace audit log records, who can read it, and how to filter it.

The audit log is a history of administrative and sensitive actions in a workspace, newest first. Open **Settings**, then **Audit log** (under **Advanced**). Owners, Admins and Auditors can read it. Nobody can change or delete an entry, and entries never contain secret values. ## What is recorded [Section titled “What is recorded”](#what-is-recorded) Each entry shows the action, who did it, what it changed, when, and whether it was **Allowed** or **Denied**. The actor is a member’s name, an API client (by the start of its ID), or **Workspace retention policy** for deletions made by retention. A participant reveal also shows the reason the member gave. | Area | Actions recorded | | ------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Workspace | Workspace created, details updated, paused and resumed, ownership transferred, Fast decisions provider changed. | | Members | Invitations sent, resent and revoked, members joined, role changes, removals, invitation policy changes. | | Sign-in and SSO | Sign-in policy changes, identity connections created, changed, activated and removed, domains added, verified and removed, SCIM settings, token rotations and provisioned users, members who joined through SSO. | | API access | API clients created, keys created and revoked. | | Secrets and integrations | Workspace secrets created, rotated and deleted; package secrets set, removed, bound and unbound; remote MCP server connections created, changed, removed and authorized. | | Conversations | Participants revealed, conversations taken over and returned, team replies sent, notes added, files opened, conversation erasures. | | Data and voice | Data retention changes, maximum call duration changes. | | Billing | Spend budgets created, changed and deleted. | | Customer identity | Changes to customer identity connections, policies and settings, account links created, and revocations. | Retention changes, call duration changes and participant reveals are recorded before they take effect. If Caplo cannot write the entry, the action is refused and the console says that nothing was changed. ## Filter the log [Section titled “Filter the log”](#filter-the-log) The page loads 50 entries at a time; select **Load more activity** for older ones. Narrow it with: * **Action**: one of the listed actions, such as **Member role changed**, **Participant revealed** or **API key revoked**. * **Resource**: the kind of thing changed, such as **Member**, **Conversation**, **API client** or **Data retention**. * **Date range**: whole days in the workspace time zone, set in [General settings](/admin/workspace-settings/). * **Search audit log**: searches the entries already loaded by actor, action, target or reason. Times are shown in the workspace time zone. The **Action** list offers the most common actions. To find others, such as secret or SSO changes, leave **Action** at **All actions** and search the loaded entries. The console has no export of the audit log. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Workspaces and roles](/concepts/workspaces-and-roles/) * [Data retention and privacy](/operate/data-and-privacy/) * [Work in the Conversations inbox](/operate/conversations-inbox/#reveal-a-participant)

# Work in the Conversations inbox

> Find and read customer conversations, step in and reply as your team, leave internal notes, reveal a participant, and send WhatsApp templates from the console.

**Conversations** (under **Monitor** in the console) lists the calls and chats your customers have on every channel. Test chats from the agent builder and the CLI never appear here. For what a conversation is and how handoff works, see [Conversations and handoff](/concepts/conversations-and-handoff/). Owners, Admins, Builders, Supervisors, Auditors and Viewers can read conversations. Only Owners, Admins and Supervisors can take over, reply, add notes and reveal participants. See [Workspaces and roles](/concepts/workspaces-and-roles/). ## Find a conversation [Section titled “Find a conversation”](#find-a-conversation) The counts at the top cover the selected date range and refresh every minute: | Count | What it counts | | ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Conversations** | All conversations started in the range. | | **Live now** | Conversations still in progress. | | **Needs attention** | Conversations that failed or whose transfer was not accepted. | | **Calls · Chats** | The same total split into voice and text. | | **First response** | The median time to the agent’s first reply. | Select a count, or use the **All**, **Live now** and **Needs attention** views. Narrow the list with: * **Calls** or **Chats**, and the date range: **Last 24 hours**, **Last 7 days**, **Last 30 days** or **All retained**. * **Filters**: **Channel type**, **Status** (**Live**, **Completed**, **Transferred**, **Failed**), **Channel**, **Agent** and its **Version**, **Has errors** and **Handed off**. * The sort: **Recent activity** or **Newest first**. The search box filters only the rows on the current page. To open any conversation, paste its full conversation ID and press Enter. When new conversations arrive, a **new conversations · Show** bar appears above the list instead of moving the rows under you. Each row shows who is handling the conversation: **AI agent**, **Your team** (taken over in the console) or **External inbox** (handed off to a destination such as Chatwoot), plus signals for errors, handoffs and the estimated model cost. ## Read a conversation [Section titled “Read a conversation”](#read-a-conversation) Select a row to open it beside the list. The header shows the channel and **Participant hidden**: the customer’s identity stays masked until someone reveals it. Below it are the agent and release, start time, length, first response time and estimated cost. * **Transcript** shows the messages from the customer, the agent and your team, with times in UTC. A voice call shows its transcript, which updates while the call is in progress, and why each call ended, such as **Caller hung up** or **Time limit reached**. * On messaging channels, your team’s and the agent’s messages show their delivery state, such as **Sent**, **Delivered**, **Read** or **Delivery failed**. * Files a customer sends while a person handles the conversation appear in the transcript as an image or a download. * If the agent collects ratings, a rated reply shows **Rated helpful by the visitor** or **Rated not helpful by the visitor**, with the visitor’s reason. See [Replies and feedback](/agents/replies-and-feedback/#where-operators-see-ratings). * **Show tool calls**, under an agent reply, lists each tool and sub-agent call behind it: its outcome, why it failed or was refused, its arguments and the result the model read. * **Activity** lists each turn with its status, latency, model calls, tokens, estimated cost, and the tools it called with their status and error. Tool details are visible to Owners, Admins and Supervisors; other roles see **Detail restricted**. To work through a wrong answer step by step, see [Monitor and analytics](/operate/monitor-and-analytics/#investigate-a-bad-answer). **More actions** offers **Download transcript** (a text file), **Copy link** and **Copy conversation ID**. ## Take over and reply [Section titled “Take over and reply”](#take-over-and-reply) You can take over an open text conversation that the agent is handling. Voice calls cannot be taken over, and neither can a conversation that is with an external inbox. 1. Open the conversation and select **Take over**. The customer sees that a member of your team has joined. 2. Type in **Reply to the customer as your team** and select **Send**. Replies are up to 4,000 characters. 3. When you are done, select **Return to agent**. While your team has the conversation: * The agent does not reply. Customer messages are recorded in the transcript and start no agent turn. * Any Owner, Admin or Supervisor can reply or return it, not only the person who took over. If someone else changed the conversation in the meantime, the console refuses the command and reloads it; check the transcript and try again. * On WhatsApp, Messenger and Instagram, replies are queued and delivered in order. These channels allow free replies only within 24 hours of the customer’s last message; the panel shows when the WhatsApp reply window closes. After **Return to agent**, the agent answers the customer’s next message. A conversation accepts new messages only while it is open. Once it ends or reaches the channel’s inactivity timeout, **Take over** and the composer disappear. On a WhatsApp, Messenger or Instagram channel whose **Who answers** setting is **Your team**, the agent stays silent and customer messages wait here for your team to take over. See [Channels](/channels/). ## Internal notes [Section titled “Internal notes”](#internal-notes) The **Notes** tab holds notes for your team, up to 4,000 characters each. The customer and the agent never see them. Notes show their author and time, and adding one is recorded in the [audit log](/operate/audit-log/). ## Reveal a participant [Section titled “Reveal a participant”](#reveal-a-participant) Owners, Admins and Supervisors can see who the customer is, such as the account ID of a verified customer. 1. Open **More actions** and select **Reveal participant · audited**. 2. Enter the reason you need to see it, up to 1,000 characters. 3. Select **Reveal**. The identity replaces **Participant hidden** for you until you reload the page. Every reveal is recorded in the workspace [audit log](/operate/audit-log/) with your reason. ## Send a WhatsApp template [Section titled “Send a WhatsApp template”](#send-a-whatsapp-template) Outside the 24-hour window, WhatsApp only delivers templates that Meta has approved. You can send one from the inbox to a conversation you have taken over: 1. Select **Take over**, then **More actions** and **Send approved template**. 2. Choose an **Approved template** and fill in each **Body parameter**. The dialog shows a **Message preview**. 3. Choose a recorded **Customer opt-in** for the template’s category. If there is none, pick the **Customer message with explicit opt-in**, check that it really grants permission, and select **Record customer opt-in**. 4. Select **Queue template**. Its delivery state appears in the transcript. Only body-text templates synchronized from your WhatsApp account are offered. **Revoke selected opt-in** withdraws a recorded opt-in. If the dialog says a previous send has an unconfirmed outcome, select **Check pending command** before you send again, so the customer does not get the template twice. For connecting WhatsApp and managing templates, see [WhatsApp](/channels/whatsapp/). Caution A booking request or an ordinary reply is not opt-in. Record opt-in only from a message in which the customer explicitly agrees to receive that category of message. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Monitor and analytics](/operate/monitor-and-analytics/) * [Handoff and end actions](/agents/handoff-and-end-actions/) * [Data and privacy](/operate/data-and-privacy/): how long transcripts are kept.

# Data retention and privacy

> What Caplo keeps about your conversations, how long transcripts and traces are kept, how to limit call length, and how conversation data is removed.

Open **Settings**, then **Data & privacy**, to choose how long Caplo keeps conversation data and how long a voice call may last. Every member can see these settings; only Owners and Admins can change them. Each change is recorded in the [audit log](/operate/audit-log/). ## What Caplo stores [Section titled “What Caplo stores”](#what-caplo-stores) | Data | What it holds | Kept for | | ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | | **Transcripts** | The messages between the customer, the agent and your team, internal notes, and the customer’s identity, which the console masks until someone [reveals](/operate/conversations-inbox/#reveal-a-participant) it. | **Transcript retention** | | **Traces** | What each turn did: steps, models, tools and sub-agents called, statuses, error codes, timings, token counts and cost estimates. The conversation list, its counts and the [analytics](/operate/monitor-and-analytics/) are built from them. | **Trace retention** | | **Test traces** | The trace data of live tests and eval runs. | 7 days | | **Usage and billing records** | Quantities and charges for your [statements](/usage/usage-and-statements/). | Kept for billing, separately from these settings | | **Audit log** | Administrative and sensitive actions. | Append-only: entries cannot be changed or deleted | ## Retention [Section titled “Retention”](#retention) | Setting | Choices | Default | Applies to | | ------------------------ | -------------------------------- | ------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Transcript retention** | 7 days, 30 days, 90 days, 1 year | 30 days | Channel conversations, counted from their last message or from when they closed. | | **Trace retention** | 7 days, 30 days, 90 days | 90 days | Production execution timelines and analytics. | 1. Open **Settings** > **Data & privacy**. 2. Under **Retention & content**, choose **Transcript retention** and **Trace retention**. 3. Save. If someone else changed retention since you opened the page, Caplo refuses the save; discard, review the current values and save again. How it takes effect: * A shorter period also applies to records you already have. Older transcripts are deleted on the next cleanup run, and older trace data within minutes. * A transcript is never deleted while its conversation is still open, and a conversation that is waiting for the outcome of a tool write is kept until that outcome is settled. * Conversations only appear in **Conversations** and in analytics while their trace data is kept. If you keep transcripts longer than traces, older conversations drop out of the list before their transcripts are deleted. Shortening retention deletes data When you shorten either period, everything older than the new period is permanently deleted at the next cleanup. Export what you need first, for example with **Download transcript** in the inbox or a CSV from the [Usage](/usage/usage-and-statements/#export) page. ## Maximum call duration [Section titled “Maximum call duration”](#maximum-call-duration) Under **Voice calls**, **Maximum call duration (minutes)** limits every voice call in the workspace, on the phone and in the website widget. Enter a whole number from 5 to 240. Until you set one, the platform default of 60 minutes applies. The caller hears a warning one minute before the limit and a goodbye when the call reaches it. The call then ends with the reason **Time limit reached** in the inbox. A change applies to calls that start after it; a call in progress keeps the limit it started with. ## Deleting conversation data [Section titled “Deleting conversation data”](#deleting-conversation-data) The console has no control to delete a single conversation or one customer’s data. Conversations are removed when their retention period ends. To shorten how long everything is kept, lower **Transcript retention** and **Trace retention**. If you need a specific conversation erased before then, for example to honor a customer’s request, contact [support](/resources/support/) with its conversation ID; **Copy conversation ID** is in the conversation’s **More actions** menu. To remove what Caplo holds about a verified customer’s link to your accounts, see [Account links and revocation](/identity/account-links-and-revocation/). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Work in the Conversations inbox](/operate/conversations-inbox/) * [Audit log](/operate/audit-log/) * [Workspaces and roles](/concepts/workspaces-and-roles/)

# Monitor agents and read analytics

> See how production conversations turn out, trace what an agent did on each turn, investigate a bad answer, and tell live figures from preview content.

Caplo records what your agents do on every turn: which models and tools they called, how long each step took, what failed and what it cost. The console shows this in three places: | Where | What it shows | Data | | -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------- | | **Overview**, **Operational outcomes** | Outcome rates, first response time and estimated cost for production conversations | Live | | **Conversations** | Each production conversation with its transcript, tool calls and turn activity | Live | | **Monitor**, beside a live test | Every step of a test chat as it happens, as a timeline, waterfall or event log | Your test only | Reading outcomes and conversations needs a role that can read conversations: Owner, Admin, Builder, Supervisor, Auditor or Viewer. For money spent, use [Usage and billing](/usage/) instead; the cost figures here are estimates. ## Operational outcomes [Section titled “Operational outcomes”](#operational-outcomes) Open **Overview**. The **Operational outcomes** card covers production conversations started in the selected range, by their current status. Filter it by **Agent**, **Channel** and **Date range**; days follow the workspace time zone set in [General settings](/admin/workspace-settings/). | Figure | Meaning | | ------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Resolved** | Completed conversations as a share of the closed ones. | | **Transferred** | Conversations handed to a person, as a share of all. | | **Failed** | Conversations whose last turn failed, as a share of all. | | **First response** | The median time to the first reply, with the 95th percentile. It includes time waiting in the queue and the whole first turn. | | **Estimated cost** | The estimated model cost of the agents’ turns in the range. It is not split by channel, so it shows a dash while a channel is selected. | **Review poor outcomes** links to the latest failed conversations, with the agent, channel and error code of each. **Needs attention** on the same page also counts failed conversations in the range and links to them. The **Conversations** page adds live counts at the top: conversations in the range, **Live now**, **Needs attention** and the median **First response**. See [Work in the Conversations inbox](/operate/conversations-inbox/). ## What is live and what is preview [Section titled “What is live and what is preview”](#what-is-live-and-what-is-preview) Only some of the **Overview** page reads live traffic: * Live: the **Conversations** tile, **Operational outcomes**, **Review poor outcomes** and the failed conversations under **Needs attention**. * Built from your configuration, not from traffic: the **Agents** and **Channels** tiles, the **Preview setup checklist** and **Channel readiness**. Badges such as **Sample connection state**, **Provider unverified** and **Live health unavailable** mark figures that do not come from live provider or health checks. * **Helpful feedback** under **Operational outcomes** shows **Unavailable**. To read ratings, open the rated conversations in **Conversations**. **Estimated cost** figures, on **Overview** and on each conversation, come from Caplo’s rate card for model calls. They can be marked partial when a model has no price, and they are not what you are charged. Charges are on the [Billing](/usage/usage-and-statements/) page. Outcomes and conversation lists are built from trace data, so they reach back only as far as the workspace’s **Trace retention**. See [Data and privacy](/operate/data-and-privacy/). ## Trace a test chat with Monitor [Section titled “Trace a test chat with Monitor”](#trace-a-test-chat-with-monitor) In the agent builder, open **Live test** and select **Expand** to see the chat and **Monitor** side by side. Monitor shows each turn of the test as it runs, with its latency, model calls and tokens. | View | Shows | | ------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Timeline** | Each turn’s steps in order: model calls, routing, tools, confirmations, sub-agents, knowledge searches and policy checks. Select a step for its details. | | **Waterfall** | Each finished turn as nested timed spans, to see where the time went. | | **Log** | The raw execution events. | Filter by agent, by step type, or with **Errors only**. A step’s details include, depending on its kind: * For a model call: the model, provider, which key paid for it, tokens in and out, and the stop reason. * For a tool: the package and version, whether it reads or changes data, the attempt, queue and run time, cold start and confirmation. * For a sub-agent, a knowledge search or a policy check: its result, the sources and chunks found, or the limit used. * For a failure: the error code, the failing component and whether it can be retried. **Copy trace ID** and **Copy step ID** give identifiers to quote to [support](/resources/support/). **Export** downloads the test’s events as JSON. See [Test an agent](/agents/testing/). ## Investigate a bad answer [Section titled “Investigate a bad answer”](#investigate-a-bad-answer) 1. **Find the conversation.** Start from **Review poor outcomes**, a customer’s rating, a conversation ID a customer gave you, or the **Conversations** filters: **Agent** and **Version**, **Has errors**, or **Status** **Failed**. 2. **Read the transcript.** Note the exact customer message that went wrong and the release in the header. 3. **Open the tool calls.** Under the agent’s reply, select **Show tool calls**. Check each call’s outcome and reason, the arguments the agent sent, and **Result the model read**. A wrong answer often comes from a tool that failed, returned the wrong data, or was never called. 4. **Check the turn.** The **Activity** tab shows the turn’s status, latency, sub-agent calls, knowledge searches and any error code with its meaning. 5. **Reproduce it.** In **Live test**, choose the **Published** release the customer used, send the same message, and follow it in **Monitor**. Tool packages have their own invocation logs; see [Versions and logs](/tools/versions-and-logs/). 6. **Fix it and keep it fixed.** Change the instructions, knowledge or tool, add the conversation as an [eval case](/agents/evals/), then [publish](/agents/publishing/) and deploy the new release. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Work in the Conversations inbox](/operate/conversations-inbox/) * [Usage and statements](/usage/usage-and-statements/) * [Write and run evals](/agents/evals/)

# Models and providers

> Choose which language model and speech providers your agents can use, and whether each one runs on Caplo's account or your own key.

Agents call two kinds of providers: language model providers, which write replies and decide which tools to call, and speech providers, which turn caller audio into text and replies into speech. For each provider your workspace chooses whether it is on, and if so, whose account pays for its calls. ## Providers [Section titled “Providers”](#providers) | Kind | Provider | Used for | | --------------- | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Language models | Anthropic | Agent replies and tool use. | | Language models | OpenAI | Agent replies and tool use, realtime voice models, and [Fast decisions](/providers/fast-decisions/). | | Speech | Deepgram | Speech recognition on voice calls. | | Speech | ElevenLabs | Voices that speak the agent’s replies. | The **Language models** list also shows Google, labelled **Realtime voice (Gemini Live) only**. Caplo does not route agent chat to Google models, so turn Google on only if an agent uses Gemini Live for realtime voice. See [Voice and speech](/providers/voice-and-speech/). The models you can pick for an agent depend on which providers are on. See [Instructions and model](/agents/instructions-and-model/) and [Voice and speech](/providers/voice-and-speech/). ## Key source [Section titled “Key source”](#key-source) Each provider has one of three settings, called its key source: | Key source | What happens | Who bills the provider usage | | ----------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | | **Off** | Agents can’t use this provider’s models. | Nobody. | | **Caplo Managed** | Calls run on Caplo’s provider account. No key needed. | Caplo, on your Caplo bill. | | **Your key** | Calls run on your own provider account, with an API key you add. | The provider, directly. | **Caplo Managed** is offered only for providers Caplo runs an account for. When it is not, the option is disabled and shows “Not available in this environment”. ### Defaults [Section titled “Defaults”](#defaults) A new workspace starts with: * **Anthropic**: **Caplo Managed**. * **OpenAI** and **Google**: **Off**. * **Deepgram** and **ElevenLabs**: **Caplo Managed**. So agents on Anthropic models and voice calls with the default speech settings work without any keys. To use OpenAI models, turn OpenAI on with either key source. ## Change a provider’s key source [Section titled “Change a provider’s key source”](#change-a-providers-key-source) 1. In the console, open **Integrations** then **Service providers**. The page has two cards: **Language models** and **Speech providers**. 2. On the provider’s row, choose **Off**, **Caplo Managed** or **Your key**. 3. Select **Apply**. If you chose **Your key** and no key is saved yet, the button reads **Apply · add key** and asks for the key first. See [Bring your own key](/providers/bring-your-own-key/). The change applies to new calls straight away; you do not need to publish the agent again. When a provider is **Off**, calls to it are refused, so move agents that use its models to another provider first. ## Who can change providers [Section titled “Who can change providers”](#who-can-change-providers) Owners, Admins and Developers can change key sources and manage keys. Other roles that can see integrations (for example Builders and Viewers) see the settings read-only. ## Billing effect [Section titled “Billing effect”](#billing-effect) * **Caplo Managed** usage appears on your Caplo bill at the provider’s list price plus your plan’s markup. * **Your key** usage is billed to you by the provider and adds no AI usage charge from Caplo. Caplo’s platform fees (per voice minute and per AI reply) still apply. See [How Caplo charges](/usage/). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Bring your own key](/providers/bring-your-own-key/) * [Fast decisions](/providers/fast-decisions/) * [Voice and speech](/providers/voice-and-speech/)

# Bring your own key

> Run a model or speech provider on your own provider account by adding an API key, and rotate or remove it later.

With **Your key**, an agent’s calls to a provider run on your own account with that provider. The provider bills you directly, and Caplo adds no AI usage charge for those calls. Use it when you already have negotiated rates, need usage on your own account, or want a provider Caplo does not manage for you. You can bring your own key for Anthropic, OpenAI, Deepgram and ElevenLabs. Each provider has one key per workspace. ## Who can manage keys [Section titled “Who can manage keys”](#who-can-manage-keys) Owners, Admins and Developers can add, replace and remove keys and change key sources. Other members who can view integrations see the key source and the last characters of a saved key, never the key itself. ## Add a key and use it [Section titled “Add a key and use it”](#add-a-key-and-use-it) 1. In the console, open **Integrations** then **Service providers**. 2. On the provider’s row, under **Language models** or **Speech providers**, choose **Your key** and select **Apply · add key**. 3. Paste the key into the provider’s **API key** field and select **Check key and use it**. Caplo checks the key with the provider before saving it. If the provider rejects it, you see “The provider did not accept this key. Check the key and try again.” and nothing changes. When the check passes, Caplo saves the key and switches the provider to **Your key** in one step. To save a key without switching to it yet, select **Add key** on the row instead, then **Check and save key**. Switch to **Your key** later with **Apply**. ## How keys are stored and shown [Section titled “How keys are stored and shown”](#how-keys-are-stored-and-shown) * Caplo stores the key encrypted and never shows it again, in the console or through any API. * The row shows **Configured · ending in** and the last 4 characters of the key, so you can tell which key is saved. Keys shorter than 16 characters show only **Configured**. * The row also shows when the key was last checked, in UTC. * Agents never see the key. It is used only for the calls Caplo makes to the provider on your workspace’s behalf. ## Rotate a key [Section titled “Rotate a key”](#rotate-a-key) 1. Create the new key in the provider’s dashboard. 2. In **Service providers**, select **Replace key** on the provider’s row. 3. Paste the new key and select **Check and save key**. The dialog shows the last characters of the key it replaces. 4. Once calls succeed with the new key, revoke the old key in the provider’s dashboard. Caplo deletes the old key when it saves the new one. New calls use the new key straight away. ## Remove a key [Section titled “Remove a key”](#remove-a-key) You can’t remove the key a provider is currently using. First switch the provider to **Off** or **Caplo Managed** and select **Apply**, then select **Remove key** and confirm. Caplo deletes the stored key. ## What happens if your key fails [Section titled “What happens if your key fails”](#what-happens-if-your-key-fails) Calls on **Your key** use only your key. If the provider rejects it, the key is revoked, or your account runs out of quota, those calls fail; Caplo does not switch them to its own account. Replace the key, or switch the provider to **Caplo Managed** if it is offered. ## Realtime voice models [Section titled “Realtime voice models”](#realtime-voice-models) A realtime voice model writes and speaks a call’s replies itself. It belongs to a language model provider, so its calls use that provider’s **Language models** key source. For example, an OpenAI realtime model runs on your OpenAI key when OpenAI is set to **Your key**, and is unavailable when OpenAI is **Off**. See [Voice and speech](/providers/voice-and-speech/). ## Billing effect [Section titled “Billing effect”](#billing-effect) * Calls on your key add no AI usage charge to your Caplo bill. Your provider bills you for them. * Caplo’s platform fees still apply to every production conversation, whoever’s key it used: per connected voice minute and per completed AI reply. * The **Billing** page counts the requests that used your keys and notes that those providers bill you directly. The **Usage** page includes their token and speech quantities; filter **Keys** by **Your keys** to see them alone. See [How Caplo charges](/usage/) and [Usage and statements](/usage/usage-and-statements/).

# Fast decisions

> Turn on OpenAI-powered fast routing for your workspace, see what data it sends, and how agents with sub-agents use it.

Fast decisions let an agent that delegates to sub-agents pick the right sub-agent with one quick decision call, instead of extra rounds of model planning. A routing decision takes about 0.3 seconds instead of about 2 seconds, so customers wait less before the right specialist starts answering. Fast decisions use the OpenAI Decisions API. Because that sends conversation content to OpenAI, it is off until an Owner or Admin accepts OpenAI’s data processing terms for the workspace. After that, each agent still chooses whether to use it. ## What is sent to OpenAI [Section titled “What is sent to OpenAI”](#what-is-sent-to-openai) When an agent uses fast routing, each routing decision sends: * the visitor’s recent messages, and * the descriptions of the agent’s sub-agents (what each one is for, and when not to use it). Agent instructions, tool results, memory and credentials are never sent. Decisions are processed in the United States, the only region offered today. They run on Caplo’s OpenAI account whatever your **OpenAI** key source is under **Integrations** then **Service providers**, and they appear on your bill as Caplo-managed usage. ## Turn on fast decisions [Section titled “Turn on fast decisions”](#turn-on-fast-decisions) 1. In the console, open **Settings** then **AI providers**. 2. In **Fast decisions**, check the **Provider** (OpenAI Decisions API) and **Processing region**. 3. Read the notice about what is sent, then select **I accept the OpenAI data processing terms for this workspace**. OpenAI becomes a subprocessor for routing decisions. 4. Select **Enable fast decisions**. The panel then shows **Enabled**, the terms version you accepted and when the setting changed. The change is recorded in the workspace audit log. Only Owners and Admins can turn fast decisions on or off. Other members see the setting read-only. ## Use fast routing in an agent [Section titled “Use fast routing in an agent”](#use-fast-routing-in-an-agent) Turning on fast decisions does not change any agent by itself. For each agent that delegates to sub-agents: 1. Open the agent’s sub-agent settings and, under **Routing**, choose **Fast routing** instead of **The model decides**. 2. Give every sub-agent link a **Do not use when** description. Fast routing can’t be saved without one on each link. 3. Optionally, under **Advanced**, set **Minimum confidence** (0.60 to 0.99, default 0.85). Below it, the agent’s model decides instead. 4. Publish a release and update the channels that should use it. When a fast decision is not confident enough, or the decision call fails, the agent’s model decides as it would without fast routing, so a conversation is never stuck on routing. See [Sub-agents and routing](/agents/sub-agents-and-routing/) for how to write good sub-agent descriptions and test routing with evaluations. ## Turn fast decisions off [Section titled “Turn fast decisions off”](#turn-fast-decisions-off) In **Settings** then **AI providers**, select **Disable fast decisions** and confirm. Agents set to fast routing fall back to model planning on their next turn, and no data is sent to OpenAI while it is off. Their routing setting is kept, so they use fast routing again if you turn it back on.

# Voice and speech

> How agents hear and speak on voice calls, where to choose speech recognition, voices and realtime models, and what affects the cost of a call.

On a voice call, whether by phone or in the website widget, an agent needs to turn the caller’s speech into text and its own replies into speech. Caplo does this with speech providers and, optionally, a realtime voice model. ## How a voice reply is made [Section titled “How a voice reply is made”](#how-a-voice-reply-is-made) **Speech pipeline** (always used): 1. **Speech recognition** transcribes the caller. 2. The agent’s language model writes the reply, the same way it does in chat. 3. A **voice model** speaks the reply in the chosen voice. **Realtime model** (optional): a realtime voice model writes and speaks each reply itself, so replies start sooner. Speech recognition still transcribes the caller, and the pipeline’s voice still speaks the greeting and the fixed lines such as the transfer and silence messages. ## Providers [Section titled “Providers”](#providers) * Speech recognition and voice models come from **Deepgram** and **ElevenLabs**, set under **Speech providers**. * Realtime models belong to a language model provider (OpenAI for OpenAI Realtime, Google for Gemini Live) and use its **Language models** key source. Both are in the console under **Integrations** then **Service providers**. Speech providers are **Caplo Managed** by default, so voice works without your own keys. See [Models and providers](/providers/). ## Where to choose them [Section titled “Where to choose them”](#where-to-choose-them) Voice settings belong to the agent, so every channel serving a release uses the same ones. Open the agent’s configuration, then **Advanced settings** then **Voice**: | Setting | What it does | | ---------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Voice language** | The language callers speak and the agent answers in. Changing it can replace speech models that do not support the new language; the console tells you when it does. | | **Speech recognition** | The model that transcribes callers. Models are grouped by provider, and each shows the key source its calls use. | | **Voice model** and **Voice** | The model and voice that speak replies. Play a preview of a voice, optionally with your own **Preview sentence**, before you choose. | | **Realtime model** and **Realtime voice** | Optional. A realtime model and one of its provider’s voices. | | Fixed lines | What the agent says when it cannot take the call, a turn fails, the call is transferred, the caller is silent or the time limit is near. Leave a line empty to use the platform’s. | | **Transcript script** | Rewrites the caller’s transcript into another script, such as Urdu script, before the agent reads it. | | **Start replies early** | Starts the reply while the caller may still be finishing, to answer sooner. | | **Silence check after** and **End call after** | Seconds of caller silence before the silence check line, and before the call ends. 5 to 300 seconds each; the platform defaults are 15 and 10. | Changes apply to new calls after you publish a release and update the channel to it. If you leave voice unset, the agent uses Caplo’s default English speech recognition and voice. Channels decide whether voice is offered: * A phone channel is always voice. * A website channel offers voice only when the channel allows website voice and the entry point’s mode is **Text + voice**. See [Website widget settings](/channels/website-widget/settings/#text-and-voice). ## What affects cost [Section titled “What affects cost”](#what-affects-cost) A voice conversation can add these charges. See [How Caplo charges](/usage/) for how each is billed. | What | Measured in | Billed by | | -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- | | Connected call time | Voice minutes, by the second | Caplo platform fee, whatever keys you use. | | Speech recognition | Audio minutes | Caplo on **Caplo Managed**; your provider on **Your key**. | | Speaking replies | Characters spoken | Caplo on **Caplo Managed**; your provider on **Your key**. | | Language model or realtime model | Text and audio tokens | Caplo on **Caplo Managed**; your provider on **Your key**. | | Phone carrier | Call time on your own Twilio or Telnyx account | Your carrier. | What makes a call cost more: * **Longer calls**: every connected minute counts, including silence before the call ends. Shorter silence timings end abandoned calls sooner. * **Longer replies**: more characters to speak and more output tokens. Ask for short spoken replies in the agent’s instructions. * **Realtime models**: they bill audio tokens for what they hear and say, on top of the pipeline’s speech recognition. The **Usage** page shows speech to text minutes, text to speech characters and voice minutes by provider, agent or channel. See [Usage and statements](/usage/usage-and-statements/).

# Build tools

> What tools are, the three ways to give agents tools, and how a tool version reaches a published agent.

A tool is a function an agent can call during a conversation: look up an order, check stock, book an appointment, cancel a subscription. Each tool has a name, a description the model reads, an input schema for its arguments and, optionally, an output schema for its result. Caplo runs the tool, checks its result against the schema, and hands the result to the agent. Every tool also has a policy that you set, not the model: * **Effect.** A `read` tool only looks things up. A `write` tool changes something, and by default runs only after the customer confirms it. * **Timeout.** From 1 to 30 seconds. * **Network and secrets.** A tool package reaches only the hosts it lists and receives only the secrets it declares. ## Three ways to provide tools [Section titled “Three ways to provide tools”](#three-ways-to-provide-tools) | Kind | What you build | Where it runs | Choose it when | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Function package | One tool, written as a TypeScript function with `defineTool` | On Caplo, in Node 24 | You need a single tool, or you are wrapping one API call. This is the quickest start. | | MCP package | An MCP server with several tools, written with the official MCP TypeScript SDK | On Caplo, in Node 24 | You have several related tools that share code, or you want the same server to work in other MCP clients. | | Remote MCP server | Nothing in Caplo; you connect an MCP server you or a vendor already runs | On that server | The tools already exist behind an HTTPS MCP endpoint, or they must run in your own infrastructure. | Function and MCP packages are both **tool packages**: a folder with a `caplo.yaml`, a `package.json` with a lockfile, and TypeScript source. You build and publish them with the `caplo` CLI or in Tool Studio, and Caplo hosts them. A remote MCP server stays where it is; Caplo calls it with the credential you configure and only for the tools you review and publish. ## How a tool reaches an agent [Section titled “How a tool reaches an agent”](#how-a-tool-reaches-an-agent) 1. You publish a tool package build, or a reviewed remote MCP tool list, as a numbered **version**. A version never changes. 2. You select tools on the agent, in the console or in agent YAML as `<package>/<tool>`, for example `orders/get_order`. 3. When you publish the agent, its release **pins** the latest published version of each package it uses. Publishing a new tool version does not change running agents. They keep calling the pinned version until you publish the agent again, which pins the newest version. Revoking a version is the exception: agents that pin it stop calling its tools at once. See [Give an agent tools](/agents/tools/) and [Agents and releases](/concepts/agents-and-releases/). The model sees each tool as `<package>__<tool>` (hyphens in the package name become underscores), so tools from different packages never collide. ## Guides [Section titled “Guides”](#guides) [Build your first tool](/tools/first-tool/)Create, test, push and publish a function package with the CLI or in Tool Studio. [Define tools and use the call context](/tools/define-tool-and-context/)defineTool, MCP packages, schemas, caplo.yaml and the per-call context. [Write tools safely](/tools/write-tools/)Effects, customer confirmation, idempotency keys, outcomes and status checks. [Secrets and network access](/tools/secrets-and-egress/)Declare secrets, set or bind their values, and allow the hosts a package calls. [Show results to customers](/tools/present-results/)Cards, choices, details, timelines, receipts, files, progress and forms. [Build customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/)Act for a verified customer and verify customer context tokens in your API. [Test tools with fixtures](/tools/testing/)Run fixtures locally and on Caplo before you publish. [Manage versions and read logs](/tools/versions-and-logs/)Builds, versions, revoking, archiving and live invocation logs. [Connect a remote MCP server](/tools/remote-mcp/)Check, connect, review and publish the tools of an MCP server you already run. [Tool limits](/tools/limits/)Runtime, timeouts, memory, sizes, network and log retention.

# Build customer-aware tools

> Let a tool act for the verified customer in the conversation, and verify Caplo's customer context token in your own API.

Some tools must act for one specific customer: “show my orders”, “change my delivery address”. The customer’s identity must not come from the model, because a model can be talked into passing someone else’s account number. A customer-aware tool instead receives the **verified customer** from Caplo, which knows who the visitor is because they signed in through your [customer identity](/channels/website-widget/settings/) setup. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * A customer identity connection in **Settings**, **Customer identity**, so visitors can verify who they are (for example with your own sign-in). * A channel whose customer verification lets or requires visitors to verify. See [Website widget settings](/channels/website-widget/settings/). ## Declare the subject [Section titled “Declare the subject”](#declare-the-subject) Add `subject` to the tool’s policy in `caplo.yaml`: 
```yaml
tools:
  - name: list_my_orders
    effect: read
    timeoutSeconds: 10
    subject:
      policy: verified_required
      selector: account
```
 * `policy: verified_required` means the tool runs only for a verified customer. Caplo passes that customer in the call context, never through arguments. Tools without `subject` never receive it. * `selector` is optional. It names a string input property that Caplo fills with the customer’s resource reference on every call. The model cannot set it: a call where the model supplied it is refused. The property must be optional in the input schema (not listed in `required`), and it can never be shown in a [presentation](/tools/present-results/). For a function package, put `subject` at the top level beside `effect`. ## Read the customer in code [Section titled “Read the customer in code”](#read-the-customer-in-code) `ctx.subject` holds the verified customer. Call `ctx.requireSubject()` to get it or fail: it throws when the call has none, so the tool can never run unscoped. 
```ts
import { defineTool } from "@caplo/tools";


export default defineTool({
  name: "list_my_orders",
  description: "List the signed-in customer's recent orders.",
  inputSchema: { type: "object", properties: {}, additionalProperties: false },
  async handler(_args, ctx) {
    const customer = ctx.requireSubject();
    const response = await fetch(`https://api.shop.example/customers/${encodeURIComponent(customer.externalSubject)}/orders`, {
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${ctx.secrets.get("SHOP_API_KEY")}` },
      signal: ctx.signal,
    });
    if (!response.ok) throw new Error(`order list failed with ${response.status}`);
    return { orders: (await response.json()).orders.slice(0, 10) };
  },
});
```
 | Property | What it is | | ----------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `externalSubject` | The customer’s ID in your system, as your sign-in asserted it (its `sub`). | | `subjectNamespace`, `subjectNamespaceRef` | The identity namespace the customer verified in. | | `resourceRef` | `subjectNamespaceRef:externalSubject`, a stable key to map to your customer account. | | `level` | How strongly the customer verified: `low`, `standard` or `high`. | | `subjectId`, `bindingId` | Caplo’s IDs for the customer and their sign-in. | | `customerToken` | A customer context token, when the channel is set to send one. See below. | Function packages announce support for the subject automatically. An MCP package must add the capability when it creates the server, or Caplo refuses to send it subject calls: 
```ts
import { SUBJECT_CONTEXT_CAPABILITY } from "@caplo/tools";


const server = new McpServer(
  { name: "accounts", version: "1.0.0" },
  { capabilities: { logging: {}, experimental: { ...SUBJECT_CONTEXT_CAPABILITY } } },
);
```
 ## Customer context tokens [Section titled “Customer context tokens”](#customer-context-tokens) Passing the customer ID to your API with a shared API key means your API trusts the tool completely. A **customer context token** lets your API check for itself that Caplo verified this customer for this call. It is a signed JWT that: * lasts 60 seconds and is meant for a single call; * names your API as its audience and your workspace’s Caplo issuer; * carries the customer’s ID in `sub`, the agent in `act`, the verification level in `acr`, and for a write, the confirmed action in `caplo.action` and `caplo.actionDigest`. ### Set it up [Section titled “Set it up”](#set-it-up) 1. In **Settings**, **Customer identity**, edit the connection your customers use and list your API under **API audiences**: one HTTPS URL per line, up to 8. Tools can only get tokens for these audiences. 2. On the same page, under **Customer context tokens**, copy the **Issuer** (and the **JWKS URL**, if your API needs it). Give them to whoever runs the API. 3. In the website channel’s settings, under **Customer verification**, find the tool’s operation in **Verified tool operations** and choose your API in its **customer token audience**. Adding an operation to a channel is not yet available in the console or the CLI: [contact support](/resources/support/) with the channel and the tools, then set the audience here. See [Customer identity](/identity/). 4. In the tool, send the token as a bearer token. It is not an enumerable property, so spreading or logging the subject does not leak it, but never log it yourself: 
   ```ts
   const customer = ctx.requireSubject();
   if (!customer.customerToken) throw new Error("no customer context token is configured for this tool");
   const response = await fetch("https://api.shop.example/me/orders", {
     headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${customer.customerToken}` },
     signal: ctx.signal,
   });
   ```
 ### Verify the token in your API [Section titled “Verify the token in your API”](#verify-the-token-in-your-api) Install `@caplo/tools` in your API and use `verifyCustomerContext` from `@caplo/tools/verify`. It has no runtime dependencies; it uses `fetch` and WebCrypto. 
```ts
import { CustomerContextError, MemoryReplayStore, verifyCustomerContext } from "@caplo/tools/verify";


const replayStore = new MemoryReplayStore();


export async function customerFrom(request: Request) {
  const token = request.headers.get("authorization")?.replace(/^Bearer /, "") ?? "";
  try {
    const claims = await verifyCustomerContext(token, {
      issuer: process.env.CAPLO_ISSUER!,          // the Issuer from Settings, Customer identity
      audience: "https://api.shop.example",       // one of the connection's API audiences
      replayStore,                                // refuse a token used twice
    });
    return { customerId: claims.sub, level: claims.acr };
  } catch (error) {
    if (error instanceof CustomerContextError) return null; // answer 401
    throw error;
  }
}
```
 `verifyCustomerContext(token, options)` refuses a token unless its type is `at+jwt`, it is signed with ES256 by a key from `<issuer>/.well-known/jwks.json`, its issuer and audience match exactly, it is valid now and lives at most 60 seconds, and, with a replay store, its `jti` has not been used. It returns the claims or throws `CustomerContextError` with a `reason` such as `issuer`, `audience`, `expired`, `signature` or `replayed`. | Option | Notes | | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `issuer` | Required. The exact issuer from the console. | | `audience` | Required. This API’s audience, as listed on the connection. | | `replayStore` | Recommended for write APIs. `MemoryReplayStore` suits a single instance; with several instances, implement `use(jti, expiresAt)` on a shared store, returning `false` for a used ID. | | `requiredScope` | A scope the token must carry. | | `clockToleranceSeconds` | Allowed clock skew, default 0. | Key sets are cached for their `Cache-Control` max-age, and an unknown key ID triggers a refetch at most every 30 seconds, so key rotation needs no change on your side. Caution Look the customer up by `claims.sub`, never by an ID in the request body. That is the point of the token: a model or a tampered request cannot change whose data your API returns. ### Ask for a stronger sign-in [Section titled “Ask for a stronger sign-in”](#ask-for-a-stronger-sign-in) When an operation needs a higher level or a more recent sign-in than the token shows, your API can answer `401` with an RFC 9470 challenge, for example `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="insufficient_user_authentication", acr_values="high", max_age="300"`. The tool passes it on with `stepUpFromChallenge`, and Caplo asks the customer to verify again. See [Ask the customer to verify again](/tools/write-tools/#ask-the-customer-to-verify-again).

# Define tools and use the call context

> Write function and MCP packages, design their schemas, read secrets and IDs from the call context, and set each tool's policy in caplo.yaml.

A tool package has two halves. The **code** defines each tool’s name, description and schemas, and does the work. **`caplo.yaml`** sets what Caplo enforces around it: effect, timeout, confirmation, network access and secrets. Caplo reads the tool list from your code when it builds the package and joins it with `caplo.yaml`; a tool without a policy, or a policy for a tool the code does not list, fails the build. ## Function packages [Section titled “Function packages”](#function-packages) A function package has exactly one tool. Its entry file (`entry` in `caplo.yaml`, `src/tool.ts` by default) default-exports a `defineTool`: 
```ts
import { defineTool } from "@caplo/tools";


type Input = { orderId: string };
type Output = { orderId: string; status: string; total: number; currency: string };


export default defineTool<Input, Output>({
  name: "get_order",
  description: "Look up an order by its number, for example SO-1002. Returns its status and total.",
  inputSchema: {
    type: "object",
    properties: { orderId: { type: "string", pattern: "^SO-[0-9]{4}$", description: "Order number" } },
    required: ["orderId"],
    additionalProperties: false,
  },
  outputSchema: {
    type: "object",
    properties: {
      orderId: { type: "string" },
      status: { type: "string", enum: ["processing", "shipped", "delivered", "cancelled"] },
      total: { type: "number" },
      currency: { type: "string" },
    },
    required: ["orderId", "status", "total", "currency"],
  },
  async handler({ orderId }, { secrets, signal, log }) {
    const response = await fetch(`https://api.shop.example/orders/${encodeURIComponent(orderId)}`, {
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${secrets.get("SHOP_API_KEY")}` },
      signal,
    });
    if (!response.ok) throw new Error(`order lookup failed with ${response.status}`);
    const order = await response.json();
    log.info("order found", { orderId, status: order.status });
    return { orderId: order.id, status: order.status, total: order.total, currency: order.currency };
  },
});
```
 | Field | Notes | | -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `name` | Lowercase letters, digits and underscores, starting with a letter, at most 48 characters. | | `description` | What the tool does and when to use it. The model chooses tools by this text, so be specific. | | `inputSchema` | A JSON Schema with `type: object`. Caplo validates arguments against it before your handler runs. | | `outputSchema` | Optional JSON Schema with `type: object`. When set, a result that does not match fails the call. It is required for [result presentation](/tools/present-results/). | | `handler(args, ctx)` | Receives the validated arguments and the [call context](#the-call-context). | | `status` | Optional, for write tools. See [Write tools safely](/tools/write-tools/#answer-status-checks). | What the handler returns becomes the tool result. A plain object becomes the structured result and its JSON text; a string becomes text. If the handler throws, the call returns the error message as an error result, and the model sees it. Write schemas for the model as much as for validation: describe each property, use `enum` for fixed choices, `pattern` or `maxLength` for identifiers, and `additionalProperties: false` so the model cannot invent arguments. Return only the fields the agent needs; results over 256 KiB fail. ## MCP packages [Section titled “MCP packages”](#mcp-packages) An MCP package is an MCP server with one or more tools. Its entry file (`src/server.ts` by default) exports `createServer()`, which returns a server from the official MCP TypeScript SDK: 
```ts
import { McpServer } from "@modelcontextprotocol/sdk/server/mcp.js";
import { context } from "@caplo/tools";
import { z } from "zod";


export function createServer() {
  // The logging capability lets context(extra).log send log lines with each call's result.
  const server = new McpServer({ name: "orders", version: "1.0.0" }, { capabilities: { logging: {} } });


  server.registerTool(
    "get_order",
    {
      description: "Look up an order by its number, for example SO-1002.",
      inputSchema: { orderId: z.string().regex(/^SO-[0-9]{4}$/) },
      outputSchema: { orderId: z.string(), status: z.string() },
    },
    async ({ orderId }, extra) => {
      const { secrets, signal, log } = context(extra);
      const response = await fetch(`https://api.shop.example/orders/${encodeURIComponent(orderId)}`, {
        headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${secrets.get("SHOP_API_KEY")}` },
        signal,
      });
      if (!response.ok) throw new Error(`order lookup failed with ${response.status}`);
      const order = await response.json();
      log.info("order found", { orderId });
      const result = { orderId: order.id, status: order.status };
      return { content: [{ type: "text", text: JSON.stringify(result) }], structuredContent: result };
    },
  );


  return server;
}
```
 Create the server with `capabilities: { logging: {} }`. Without it, log lines are dropped. Caplo ignores MCP tool annotations such as `readOnlyHint`; the effect comes from `caplo.yaml`. ### Run the server in other MCP clients [Section titled “Run the server in other MCP clients”](#run-the-server-in-other-mcp-clients) `caplo tool init` also writes `src/stdio.ts`, which runs the package over standard input and output with `serveStdio`. The same code then works in MCP Inspector, Claude Desktop or any other local MCP client: 
```ts
import { serveStdio } from "@caplo/tools";
import { createServer } from "./server.ts";


await serveStdio(createServer, { secrets: ["SHOP_API_KEY"] });
```
 `serveStdio` reads the listed secrets from environment variables of the same name. That is for local development only; on Caplo, secrets never come from the environment. `caplo tool dev --no-inspector` starts this file with Node 24. For a function package, `src/stdio.ts` calls `serveStdio(() => tool.createServer())`. ## The call context [Section titled “The call context”](#the-call-context) A function handler receives the context as its second argument. In an MCP package, call `context(extra)` with the handler’s `extra` argument. | Property | What it is | | ------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `secrets.get(name)` | The value of a secret declared in `caplo.yaml`. Throws if the call has no value for it. `secrets.has(name)` checks first. See [Secrets and network access](/tools/secrets-and-egress/). | | `log.debug`, `log.info`, `log.warn`, `log.error` | `(message, data?)`. Lines are kept with the invocation and shown by `caplo tool logs` and in test output. A log call never fails the tool. | | `signal` | An `AbortSignal` that fires at the deadline or when the call is cancelled. Pass it to `fetch` and other I/O. | | `deadline` | A `Date`: when the call times out. | | `idempotencyKey` | Write tools only. The same key for every attempt of one confirmed action. See [Write tools safely](/tools/write-tools/#use-the-idempotency-key). | | `invocationId` | This call’s ID, also shown in `caplo tool logs`. | | `conversationId`, `turnId` | The conversation and turn the call belongs to. | | `workspaceId`, `packageVersionId` | The workspace and the package version running. | | `subject`, `requireSubject()` | The verified customer, for tools that declare `subject`. See [Build customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/). | | `canElicit`, `elicit()` | Function packages: ask the visitor for missing details. See [Ask for missing details](/tools/present-results/#ask-for-missing-details). | Caution Module-level variables persist between calls that run in the same worker, and those calls can belong to different conversations and customers. Never keep per-customer data in module scope. Caches of public data, such as a parsed price list, are fine. `console.log` output is not part of your invocation logs. Use `log` from the context. ## caplo.yaml [Section titled “caplo.yaml”](#caployaml) | Field | Required | Notes | | ------------- | -------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `apiVersion` | Yes | `v1` (also `v1.0` or `v1.0.0`). | | `kind` | Yes | `ToolPackage`. | | `name` | Yes | Lowercase letters, digits and dashes, starting with a letter, 2 to 40 characters. Unique in the workspace and used in tool names. | | `type` | Yes | `function` or `mcp`. | | `description` | No | Up to 500 characters. | | `entry` | Yes | The entry module, for example `src/tool.ts` or `src/server.ts`. | | `runtime` | Yes | `node24`. | | `tools` | MCP only | One policy entry per tool the server lists, up to 64. | | `egress` | No | Hosts the package may call. Empty or absent means no network. | | `secrets` | No | Names of secrets the package reads. | | `tests` | No | Glob of fixture files, default `tests/*.json`. | Each `tools` entry, or for a function package the same fields at the top level: | Field | Required | Notes | | ---------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `name` | MCP only | The tool name as the server lists it. | | `effect` | Yes | `read` or `write`. | | `timeoutSeconds` | Yes | 1 to 30. | | `confirmation` | No | Write tools only: `required` (the default) or `none`. A read tool with `confirmation` fails the build. | | `subject` | No | The tool acts for a verified customer. See [Build customer-aware tools](/tools/customer-aware-tools/). | | `presentation` | No | What the customer sees: confirmation fields, receipts, result layouts and files. See [Show results to customers](/tools/present-results/). | | `progress` | No | A status line while the tool runs. | | `elicitation` | No | Read tools only: fields the tool may ask the visitor for. | A complete MCP package with a read tool and a write tool: 
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ToolPackage
name: orders
type: mcp
description: Order lookup and cancellation through the Shop API
entry: src/server.ts
runtime: node24
tools:
  - name: get_order
    effect: read
    timeoutSeconds: 5
    progress:
      label: Looking up your order
      spoken: Let me find that order.
  - name: cancel_order
    effect: write
    timeoutSeconds: 15
    confirmation: required
egress:
  - api.shop.example
secrets:
  - SHOP_API_KEY
tests: tests/*.json
```
 For a function package, leave out `tools` and put the policy beside `name`: 
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ToolPackage
name: order-lookup
type: function
entry: src/tool.ts
runtime: node24
effect: read
timeoutSeconds: 5
egress:
  - api.shop.example
secrets:
  - SHOP_API_KEY
```
 Every field is listed in the [caplo.yaml reference](/reference/caplo-yaml/). For completion and validation in your editor, add `# yaml-language-server: $schema=https://docs.caplo.si/schemas/caplo.schema.json` as the first line. ## Tool names [Section titled “Tool names”](#tool-names) The model sees each tool as `<package>__<tool>`, with dashes in the package name turned into underscores: the `get_order` tool of `orders` is `orders__get_order`. That name must be at most 64 characters, or the build fails with `tool_name_invalid`. Agents and agent YAML refer to the tool as `orders/get_order`.

# Build your first tool

> Create a function package, run it locally, test it, then push and publish it with the CLI or in Tool Studio.

This page takes a one-tool function package from nothing to a published version an agent can use. The starter tool, `double_value`, doubles a number; replace its body with your own logic once the loop works. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * The **Owner**, **Admin** or **Developer** role in the workspace. These roles can create, build and publish tools. * For the CLI path: the `caplo` CLI ([install it](/cli/install/)), signed in with `caplo auth login` and with a workspace selected with `caplo workspace use`. See [Sign in from the CLI](/cli/authentication/). * For the CLI path: **Node.js 24** and npm. Tool packages run on Node 24, and `caplo tool test` refuses any other major version. Local tests run on macOS and Linux; on Windows, use WSL. ## Create, test and publish [Section titled “Create, test and publish”](#create-test-and-publish) * CLI 1. **Create the package.** `caplo tool init` writes a package from the SDK template into a new or empty directory and names it after the directory: 
     ```sh
     caplo tool init double-value --type function
     cd double-value
     ```
 You get `caplo.yaml`, `package.json`, `tsconfig.json`, `src/tool.ts` (the tool), `src/stdio.ts` (runs it for local MCP clients) and `tests/double-value.json` (a fixture). Use `--type mcp` for an MCP package with several tools; see [Define tools and use the call context](/tools/define-tool-and-context/). 2. **Install dependencies.** 
     ```sh
     npm install
     ```
 This also writes `package-lock.json`. Keep it and commit it: Caplo installs exactly what the lockfile lists, and a push without a lockfile fails to build. `yarn.lock` and `pnpm-lock.yaml` also work. 3. **Look at the tool.** `src/tool.ts` exports one `defineTool`: 
     ```ts
     import { defineTool } from "@caplo/tools";


     export default defineTool<{ value: number }, { result: number }>({
       name: "double_value",
       description: "Doubles a number",
       inputSchema: { type: "object", properties: { value: { type: "number" } }, required: ["value"] },
       outputSchema: { type: "object", properties: { result: { type: "number" } }, required: ["result"] },
       async handler({ value }, { log }) {
         log.info("doubling", { value });
         return { result: value * 2 };
       },
     });
     ```
 `caplo.yaml` names the package and sets the tool’s policy: `effect: read` and `timeoutSeconds: 5`. 4. **Call it by hand.** `caplo tool dev` opens MCP Inspector (started with `npx`) connected to your package, so you can list and call the tool: 
     ```sh
     caplo tool dev
     ```
 Secrets your `caplo.yaml` declares are read from environment variables of the same name. To use the package from another local MCP client instead, run `caplo tool dev --no-inspector`, which speaks MCP on standard input and output. 5. **Run the fixtures.** `caplo tool test` bundles the package the way Caplo’s builder does, checks `caplo.yaml` against the tools the code lists, and runs every fixture in `tests/`: 
     ```sh
     caplo tool test
     ```
 
     ```txt
     bundle 7.5 KB (d64251e71094, esbuild 0.28.2)
     tool double_value__double_value (read, 5s)
     capabilities subject-context/1 effect-outcome/1 operation-status/1
     server advertises subject-context/1 effect-outcome/1
     ok   tests/double-value.json (doubles 21)
     1 passed, 0 failed
     ```
 `double_value__double_value` is the name the model sees. See [Test tools with fixtures](/tools/testing/). 6. **Push and build.** `caplo tool push` uploads the folder as the package’s workspace draft (creating the package on the first push), builds it, and streams the build log. `node_modules`, `.git` and anything your `.gitignore` files ignore are left out. 
     ```sh
     caplo tool push
     ```
 The build installs from your lockfile, bundles the code, runs safety checks and lists the tools with no network access. It ends with `Build <id> succeeded` and the tools it found, or with an error code such as `lockfile_required` or `tool_policy_missing`. 7. **Publish.** `caplo tool publish` turns the latest succeeded build into the next version: 
     ```sh
     caplo tool publish
     ```
 
     ```txt
     Published double-value version 1 from build <BUILD_ID>
       double_value__double_value (read, 5s)
     ```
 * Tool Studio Tool Studio is a browser editor at [studio.caplo.si](https://studio.caplo.si) for writing, building, testing and publishing tool packages without installing anything. 1. In the console, open **Tools**, select **Add tools**, then **Create package**. Studio opens on this workspace in a new tab; sign in if it asks. 2. In the **Packages** view, select **Create Package**. Choose **Function** (one tool written as a function) or **MCP server** (several tools in one server), then enter a package name such as `double-value`: a lowercase letter, then lowercase letters, digits or dashes. 3. Studio creates the package from the same template as the CLI. Edit `src/tool.ts` and `caplo.yaml`. Your edits are saved to the package’s workspace draft. When you save `package.json`, Studio resolves the dependencies and updates the lockfile for you. 4. In the **Tools** view, select **Build**. When the build succeeds, Studio offers **Run Tests** and **Publish**. To call a tool by hand, select **Call Tool** on it, fill in the arguments and select **Send Call**. 5. Select **Run Fixture Tests** to run the files in `tests/`. 6. In the **Versions** view, select **Publish** and confirm. Studio builds the draft first if it changed since the last build. You can return to a package from the console: **Tools** lists each package with **Open in Studio**. ## Give the tool to an agent [Section titled “Give the tool to an agent”](#give-the-tool-to-an-agent) A published tool appears on every agent’s **Tools & actions** tab, grouped under its package. Select it there, or list it in agent YAML by package and tool name: 
```yaml
tools:
  - double-value/double_value
```
 Then publish the agent. Its release pins version 1 of `double-value`; later versions reach the agent when you publish the agent again. See [Give an agent tools](/agents/tools/). ## Next steps [Section titled “Next steps”](#next-steps) * Replace the starter with real logic that calls your API: [Define tools and use the call context](/tools/define-tool-and-context/) and [Secrets and network access](/tools/secrets-and-egress/). * For tools that change data, read [Write tools safely](/tools/write-tools/) before you publish. ## Let an AI agent do it [Section titled “Let an AI agent do it”](#let-an-ai-agent-do-it) If you already have a REST API, a coding agent can wrap it as a tool package with read and write tools, declared secrets and an egress list. AI prompt Wrap my REST API as a Caplo tool package Builds an MCP tool package with read and write tools for an existing REST API, with declared secrets, an egress list and fixtures.
